Graphics backends

How zimr draws on the GPU through the browser's WebGPU and, outside the browser, through Vulkan: on Windows and Linux, then Android, and on macOS and iOS through Metal. The document follows the code in src/wgpu.zig and the hosts beneath it, and it is a course in graphics backend development as much as a description of zimr: each layer is explained together with the reasons for its shape. Chapters appear as the code they describe does; a chapter about a platform that is not built yet describes its design, and says so.

Code is shown in folds, generated from the source files on every build, so it is always the code that ships. A closed fold opens with a click; the prose reads straight through without opening any.

1. Overview

1.1 Platforms and APIs

A program does not talk to a GPU directly. It talks to an API that the platform provides, and the GPU vendor's driver implements that API. Each platform offers a different set: a browser offers WebGPU (and the older WebGL); Windows offers Direct3D 12 and Vulkan; Linux and Android offer Vulkan and OpenGL; Apple's platforms offer Metal, and Vulkan only through MoltenVK, a library that translates Vulkan into Metal. No single API reaches every platform, so an engine that runs everywhere needs more than one backend: the part of the engine that turns its drawing into one API's calls.

zimr uses two APIs, with a third in reserve:

PlatformAPIWindowShaders handed to the APIState
Browsers: desktop, Android, iOSWebGPU<canvas> WGSL, generated from SPIR-VRuns
WindowsVulkanWin32SPIR-V, legalized for Vulkan Built; the Vulkan host runs under the validation layer
LinuxVulkanX11 through XCB, then WaylandSPIR-V The Vulkan host runs without a window; windows designed
AndroidVulkanNativeActivitySPIR-VDesigned
macOS, iOSVulkan through MoltenVK, or MetalCAMetalLayer SPIR-V, or Metal Shading LanguageDesigned

1.2 API choice by platform

In a browser the choice is made for us: WebGPU is the only modern GPU API a web page can use. WebGPU was designed after Vulkan, Metal and Direct3D 12, as a portable subset of the three, and its concepts - devices, queues, command encoders, pipelines, bind groups - map onto each of them. That is why zimr's own GPU API has WebGPU's shape: on the web it is a thin layer over the real thing, and everywhere else it names concepts the native APIs already have.

Outside the browser, Vulkan is the closest relative of WebGPU that runs on most platforms: one Vulkan backend covers Windows, Linux and Android. Vulkan also takes shaders as SPIR-V, the binary intermediate language zimr's shader compiler produces, so no translation is needed there. Apple is the exception: its platforms run Vulkan only through MoltenVK, and if MoltenVK proves insufficient, a Metal backend needs shaders in the Metal Shading Language.

1.3 The layer stack

The engine draws through one API, wgpu.zig, which has WebGPU's objects and calls. Beneath it sits a host: the code that carries each call to a real GPU API. There is one host per GPU API, and the build chooses which one a program contains:

   engine code: renderer_2d, gpu.zig, kompute, the examples
                          |
                      wgpu.zig            one WebGPU-shaped API: handles, descriptors, calls
                          |
             +------------+-------------+
             |                          |
         web host                  Vulkan host          chosen at compile time
     (bridge.zig and          (wgpu_vulkan.zig)
       JavaScript)                      |
             |                vk.zig, win32.zig, native_os.zig
          WebGPU                        |
      (the browser)              the Vulkan driver

Nothing above wgpu.zig names a platform. The engine creates a buffer, records a render pass and submits it in the same words whether the program runs in a browser tab or as a Windows executable.

1.4 API differences

The three APIs agree on the large concepts and differ in what they promise. The differences decide most of what a backend has to do:

WebGPUVulkanMetal
Destroying an objectAllowed while the GPU still uses it; the implementation keeps it aliveOnly after the GPU has finished every command that uses it Objects are reference-counted; a command buffer retains what it uses
SynchronizationImplicit: the implementation tracks hazards and inserts barriersExplicit: the program declares barriers, image layouts and semaphores Mostly implicit, with explicit fences available
Image layoutsNoneEvery image is in a layout, changed by barriers None
New memoryReads as zerosUndefined Buffers are zero-filled; textures are undefined
ShadersWGSLSPIR-VMetal Shading Language
Resource bindingBind groupsDescriptor sets Argument buffers, or per-slot binding
PresentingDraw into the canvas's current texture Acquire a swapchain image, present it, with semaphores between Draw into a layer's drawable and present it
Clip-space YUpDownUp
Depth range0 to 10 to 10 to 1

The rows explain the size of each host. The web host is small: the browser already gives every WebGPU guarantee. The Vulkan host is large, because Vulkan guarantees almost nothing on its own, and most of its code provides, on top of Vulkan, what WebGPU promises: objects that may be destroyed at any time, ordering without declared barriers, memory that starts as zeros, and Y pointing up.

1.5 Terminology

Graphics APIs share a vocabulary that is rarely explained in one place. Each term below is defined again, with its details, where the document first needs it; this table is for looking one up.

TermWhat it isWhere
Physical device, adapterOne GPU the system offers - or a software device that acts as one 6.4
DeviceThe program's connection to one GPU; it creates every other object 6.5
QueueWhere the program hands the GPU work to run, in order7.3
Command buffer, command encoderA recorded list of GPU commands, handed to the queue as a unit7.3
SubmissionOne hand-over of command buffers to the queue; zimr numbers each one 3.3
BufferA block of GPU memory: vertices, indices, uniforms, anything else 8.1
Texture, imageA grid of texels: a picture, a render target, a depth buffer 8.4
ViewHow a shader or a pass sees a texture: which mip levels and layers, read as what 8.4
SamplerHow a shader reads a texture: the filtering, and what happens past its edges 8.6
Shader moduleCompiled shader code: WGSL for WebGPU, SPIR-V for Vulkan 5
Bind group, descriptor setThe resources a draw's shaders read, gathered under one handle 9.1
Layout (of a bind group, of a pipeline)The shape a bind group must have - which bindings, of which kinds - without the resources themselves9.1
PipelineEverything about drawing that is fixed in advance: the shaders, the vertex format, depth testing, blending9.4
Render passA stretch of drawing into a fixed set of target textures 10.1
Surface, swapchainA window's drawable area, and the few images that take turns being drawn and shown in it7.5
BarrierA rule that orders GPU work and makes earlier writes visible to later reads 7.1
Image layoutHow an image's texels are arranged in memory for a particular use 7.1
SemaphoreA signal from one GPU operation to another7.5
Timeline semaphoreA counter the GPU advances as submissions finish 3.3
Staging memoryMemory the CPU can write, that data crosses on its way to the GPU 8.2
SPIR-V, WGSLThe shader languages of Vulkan and of WebGPU: binary, and text 5
Validation layerVulkan's checker, which tests every call against the specification 6.3

2. The API and its hosts

2.1 The GPU API: wgpu.zig

Every GPU object is named by a handle: a number that the host maps to the real object. Each kind of object has its own handle type, so a buffer cannot be passed where a texture is expected, and the value 0 means "no object":

pub const BufferHandle = enum(u32) { invalid = 0, _ };

What an object should be is described by a plain struct, as in WebGPU's descriptors:

BufferDesc
/// Create a buffer.  Caller owns the returned handle.
pub const BufferDesc = struct {
    size: u64,
    usage: BufferUsage,
    label: []const u8 = "",
};

and a call turns the description into an object. The call itself does almost nothing: it hands the description to whichever host the program was built with, through gpu_host:

createBuffer
pub fn createBuffer(device: DeviceHandle, desc: BufferDesc) BufferHandle {
    if (comptime !host.has_gpu) {
        return .invalid;
    }
    bumpHandle(.buffer, 1);
    return @fromBackingInt(@intCast(gpu_host.deviceCreateBuffer(
        @backingInt(device),
        desc.size,
        @bitCast(desc.usage),
        desc.label.ptr,
        desc.label.len,
    )));
}

2.2 Host selection

A program contains exactly one host, chosen when it is built. host.zig reduces the choice to a few facts the rest of the engine may ask about - which host, whether there is a GPU, whether there is a window, and whether the program calls its own frames - plus the allocator that suits the platform:

pub const Kind = enum { web, native, headless, none };
chosenKind
/// The build's choice when it makes one; otherwise web for wasm and none for everything else.
fn chosenKind() Kind {
    if (@hasDecl(build_options, "host")) {
        return stringToEnum(Kind, build_options.host) orelse
            @compileError("build_options.host must be \"web\", \"native\", \"headless\" or \"none\"");
    }
    // lint:off arch-check: a wasm build runs in the browser unless told otherwise
    return if (builtin.cpu.arch.isWasm()) .web else .none;
}

The kind headless is a native program without a window: the Vulkan host draws into a screen of its own (13.14), and the program calls its frames itself - a counted number of them, on a clock it sets. The kind none is a build for the machine that runs the unit tests: it has no GPU, and every GPU call compiles to nothing. Code that would otherwise test the processor architecture to decide whether it may draw asks host.has_gpu instead, so the same code is right in a browser, in a native program, and in a test.

2.3 The host switch

Every call in wgpu.zig reaches its host through one declaration. Because host.kind is known at compile time, the switch resolves before the program exists: a web build contains only the web host, a native build only the Vulkan host.

/// The host every wrapper calls, chosen at compile time: WebGPU through the browser bridge on the web, Vulkan
/// natively. Without a GPU (host tests, tools) each wrapper returns its invalid default before reaching it.
const gpu_host = switch (host.kind) {
    .native, .headless => vulkan_host,
    .web, .none => web_host,
};

2.4 The web host

In a browser, a WebAssembly module cannot call WebGPU itself: WebGPU is a JavaScript API. So the web host is a list of imported functions, one per call, which the page's JavaScript provides. Each takes numbers - handles, sizes, pointers into the module's memory - because numbers are all that cross the boundary between WebAssembly and JavaScript:

web_host
/// The browser's side of each call, under the host-neutral name every GPU host declares: the extern's name without
/// `js_`. On the web `gpu_host` IS this namespace, so every wrapper's call compiles to the same wasm import as before.
const web_host = struct {
    pub const initDevice = js_init_device;
    pub const deviceGetQueue = js_device_get_queue;
    pub const getSurface = js_get_surface;
    pub const surfaceGetCurrentTexture = js_surface_get_current_texture;
    pub const surfacePresent = js_surface_present;
    pub const surfaceGetFormat = js_surface_get_format;
    pub const surfaceGetSize = js_surface_get_size;
    pub const surfaceGetCssSize = js_surface_get_css_size;
    pub const nowMs = js_now_ms;
    pub const gpuMsLast = js_gpu_ms_last;
    pub const deviceCreateBuffer = js_device_create_buffer;
    pub const bufferDestroy = js_buffer_destroy;
    pub const queueWriteBuffer = js_queue_write_buffer;
    pub const deviceCreateTexture = js_device_create_texture;
    pub const textureCreateView = js_texture_create_view;
    pub const textureCreateViewMip = js_texture_create_view_mip;
    pub const textureCreateViewArray = js_texture_create_view_array;
    pub const textureCreateViewLayer = js_texture_create_view_layer;
    pub const textureDestroy = js_texture_destroy;
    pub const bindGroupDestroy = js_bind_group_destroy;
    pub const bindGroupLayoutDestroy = js_bind_group_layout_destroy;
    pub const pipelineLayoutDestroy = js_pipeline_layout_destroy;
    pub const renderPipelineDestroy = js_render_pipeline_destroy;
    pub const computePipelineDestroy = js_compute_pipeline_destroy;
    pub const samplerDestroy = js_sampler_destroy;
    pub const shaderModuleDestroy = js_shader_module_destroy;
    pub const textureViewDestroy = js_texture_view_destroy;
    pub const queueWriteTexture = js_queue_write_texture;
    pub const queueWriteTextureRegion = js_queue_write_texture_region;
    pub const deviceCreateSampler = js_device_create_sampler;
    pub const deviceCreateShaderModuleWgsl = js_device_create_shader_module_wgsl;
    pub const deviceCreateBindGroupLayout = js_device_create_bind_group_layout;
    pub const deviceCreateBindGroup = js_device_create_bind_group;
    pub const deviceCreatePipelineLayout = js_device_create_pipeline_layout;
    pub const deviceCreateRenderPipeline = js_device_create_render_pipeline;
    pub const deviceCreateComputePipeline = js_device_create_compute_pipeline;
    pub const deviceCreateCommandEncoder = js_device_create_command_encoder;
    pub const commandEncoderFinish = js_command_encoder_finish;
    pub const encoderCopyBufferToBuffer = js_encoder_copy_buffer_to_buffer;
    pub const bufferReadStart = js_buffer_read_start;
    pub const bufferReadPoll = js_buffer_read_poll;
    pub const bufferReadInto = js_buffer_read_into;
    pub const bufferReadRelease = js_buffer_read_release;
    pub const adapterInfo = js_adapter_info;
    pub const encoderCopyTextureToBuffer = js_encoder_copy_texture_to_buffer;
    pub const queueSubmit = js_queue_submit;
    pub const encoderBeginRenderPass = js_encoder_begin_render_pass;
    pub const encoderBeginRenderPassMrt = js_encoder_begin_render_pass_mrt;
    pub const renderPassSetPipeline = js_render_pass_set_pipeline;
    pub const renderPassSetBindGroup = js_render_pass_set_bind_group;
    pub const renderPassSetVertexBuffer = js_render_pass_set_vertex_buffer;
    pub const renderPassSetIndexBuffer = js_render_pass_set_index_buffer;
    pub const renderPassDraw = js_render_pass_draw;
    pub const renderPassDrawIndexed = js_render_pass_draw_indexed;
    pub const renderPassSetScissorRect = js_render_pass_set_scissor_rect;
    pub const renderPassEnd = js_render_pass_end;
    pub const encoderBeginComputePass = js_encoder_begin_compute_pass;
    pub const computePassSetPipeline = js_compute_pass_set_pipeline;
    pub const computePassSetBindGroup = js_compute_pass_set_bind_group;
    pub const computePassDispatchWorkgroups = js_compute_pass_dispatch_workgroups;
    pub const computePassEnd = js_compute_pass_end;
};

On the other side, the bridge keeps every WebGPU object in a table, indexed by the handle it gave out, and translates each call into the WebGPU call it stands for:

jsDeviceCreateBuffer
fn jsDeviceCreateBuffer(
    _: f64,
    size: f64,
    usage: f64,
    label_ptr: f64,
    label_len: f64,
) f64 {
    const desc: Value = global().get("Object").new(.{});
    desc.set("size", Value{ .h = js_num(size) });
    desc.set("usage", Value{ .h = js_num(@floatFromInt(decodeBufferUsage(@trunc(usage)))) });
    desc.set("label", modString(label_ptr, label_len));
    const buf: Value = g.boot.gpu_device.call("createBuffer", .{desc});
    return @floatFromInt(tblInsert(buf));
}

2.5 The Vulkan host

Natively, the same call reaches wgpu_vulkan.zig, which implements every call with Vulkan. Its functions have the web host's names and parameters, so wgpu.zig calls them in the same words. The buffer call shows the pattern of the whole host: a thin entry point that unpacks the call's arguments and reports failure by name, and a function beneath it that does the Vulkan work.

device_create_buffer
/// A buffer that reads as zeros, as WebGPU promises (`createBuffer`).
pub fn deviceCreateBuffer(
    device: u32,
    size: u64,
    usage: u32,
    label_ptr: [*]const u8,
    label_len: usize,
) u32 {
    _ = device;
    const self: *Host = theHost();
    const label: []const u8 = label_ptr[0..label_len];
    return createBuffer(self, size, @bitCast(usage), label) catch |err| {
        panic("vulkan host: creating buffer `{s}` ({d} bytes) failed: {s}", .{ label, size, @errorName(err) });
    };
}
createBuffer
/// A buffer in device memory - or, for one the CPU reads back (`map_read`), in host-visible, cached memory mapped for
/// its whole life - filled with zeros first, because WebGPU guarantees a new buffer reads as zeros and Vulkan memory
/// starts undefined.
fn createBuffer(
    self: *Host,
    size: u64,
    usage: gpu_abi.BufferUsage,
    label: []const u8,
) BringUpError!u32 {
    // Vulkan refuses a zero size; four-byte multiples suit fills and copies.
    const buffer_size: u64 = @max(std.mem.alignForward(u64, size, 4), 4);
    const buffer_info: vk.BufferCreateInfo = .{
        .size = buffer_size,
        .usage = bufferUsageFlags(usage),
        .sharing_mode = .exclusive,
    };
    var buffer: vk.Buffer = .null_handle;
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.createBuffer(self.device, &buffer_info, null, &buffer));
    var requirements: vk.MemoryRequirements = .{};
    self.device_dispatch.getBufferMemoryRequirements(self.device, buffer, &requirements);
    // Memory: device-local, for the GPU's use; for a buffer the CPU reads back, host-visible memory - cached, so the
    // CPU's reads are fast, and coherent, so the GPU's writes reach the CPU without an invalidate call - mapped once
    // for the buffer's life.
    const readable: bool = usage.map_read;
    const required: vk.MemoryPropertyFlags = .{ .host_visible = readable };
    const preferred: vk.MemoryPropertyFlags = if (readable)
        .{ .host_cached = true, .host_coherent = true }
    else
        .{ .device_local = true };
    const memory: vk.DeviceMemory = try allocateMemory(self, requirements, required, preferred, label);
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.bindBufferMemory(self.device, buffer, memory, 0));
    var mapped: ?[*]u8 = null;
    if (readable) {
        var address: ?*anyopaque = null;
        try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.mapMemory(self.device, memory, 0, vk.whole_size, .{}, &address));
        mapped = @ptrCast(address);
    }
    // Zeros, as WebGPU promises for a new buffer: a fill in `uploads`, which runs before anything submitted after.
    const uploads: vk.CommandBuffer = try uploadsCommandBuffer(self);
    fullBarrier(self, uploads);
    self.device_dispatch.cmdFillBuffer(uploads, buffer, 0, vk.whole_size, 0);
    nameObject(self, .buffer, @backingInt(buffer), label);
    return self.buffers.add(self.gpa, .{ .buffer = buffer, .memory = memory, .size = buffer_size, .mapped = mapped });
}

The Vulkan version does what the browser does silently: it creates the buffer, finds memory of the right kind for it, and fills the buffer with zeros, because WebGPU promises that a new buffer reads as zeros and Vulkan promises nothing. Chapters 6 to 10 follow the host through each kind of object.

2.6 The interface check

Two hosts that are meant to be interchangeable drift apart unless something compares them. In a web build, a compile-time check walks the web host's declarations and requires the Vulkan host to have each one, with exactly the same parameter types and return type:

/// `Host` declares each of `web_host`'s calls with the same parameter types and result type (calling convention
/// aside: the browser's are wasm imports).
fn checkHostInterface(comptime Host: type) void {
    for (@typeInfo(web_host).@"struct".decl_names) |name| {
        if (!@hasDecl(Host, name)) {
            @compileError("the Vulkan host does not declare `" ++ name ++ "`");
        }
        const browser: std.lang.Type.Fn = @typeInfo(@TypeOf(@field(web_host, name))).@"fn";
        const declared: std.lang.Type.Fn = @typeInfo(@TypeOf(@field(Host, name))).@"fn";
        if (browser.param_types.len != declared.param_types.len) {
            @compileError("the Vulkan host's `" ++ name ++ "` takes a different number of parameters");
        }
        for (browser.param_types, declared.param_types) |browser_param, declared_param| {
            if (browser_param.? != declared_param.?) {
                @compileError("the Vulkan host's `" ++ name ++ "` takes a parameter of another type");
            }
        }
        if (browser.return_type.? != declared.return_type.?) {
            @compileError("the Vulkan host's `" ++ name ++ "` returns another type");
        }
    }
}
The check, run in every web build
comptime {
    // Every call the browser takes, the Vulkan host declares with the same parameters and result, so a call it lacks
    // fails the build by name. Checked in web builds only: anywhere else, naming an
    // `extern "wgpu"` function - even only for its type - would make the program link against a library "wgpu".
    if (host.kind == .web) {
        checkHostInterface(vulkan_host);
    }
}

A call added to one host and not the other, or a parameter whose type changed on one side, stops the build with an error that names the call. The two hosts cannot disagree about the interface without the compiler saying so.

The Vulkan host also declares the calls it does not provide yet, each with its exact signature, so the check passes; the body of each is a compile-time error naming the call. Zig compiles a function's body only when something calls it, so a program that never makes such a call builds and runs, and a program that makes one fails to build, with an error naming the call it needs.

The interface check covers the Zig side of the web host. Its other side, the functions the bridge provides to the wasm, is checked as each page is built. A page's wasm lists every host function it calls in its import section, by module and name. A browser binds each import to the function the bridge registered under that name when it loads the page, and refuses the whole page if one is missing - at load time, on the user's device. The build therefore reads the import section of every page's wasm, and the names the bridge registers, and fails on any import that has no function behind it:

   the page's wasm: its imports           the bridge: its registrations
   ----------------------------------     -----------------------------------------------------
   dom.js_set_title                --->   dom.set("js_set_title", funcNum(&jsSetTitle))
   wasi_snapshot_preview1.fd_write --->   ZimrWasi's list of names, registered in a loop
   dom.js_is_fullscreen            --->   none: the build fails, naming the import
/// Every function the module `wasm` imports, in its order; the other imports - memories, tables, globals, tags - are
/// skipped.
fn functionImports(gpa: Allocator, wasm: []const u8) (ParseError || Allocator.Error)![]const Import {
    if (wasm.len < header_length or !eql(u8, wasm[0..4], "\x00asm")) {
        return error.NotWasm;
    }
    var imports: ArrayList(Import) = .empty;
    var cursor: usize = header_length;
    while (cursor < wasm.len) {
        const section_id: u8 = try readByte(wasm, &cursor);
        const section_size: u32 = try readUnsigned(wasm, &cursor);
        if (section_size > wasm.len - cursor) {
            return error.Truncated;
        }
        const section_end: usize = cursor + section_size;
        if (section_id == import_section_id) {
            const section: []const u8 = wasm[0..section_end];
            var at: usize = cursor;
            const count: u32 = try readUnsigned(section, &at);
            for (0..count) |_| {
                const module: []const u8 = try readName(section, &at);
                const name: []const u8 = try readName(section, &at);
                const kind: ImportKind = @fromBackingInt(@intCast(try readByte(section, &at)));
                switch (kind) {
                    .function => {
                        _ = try readUnsigned(section, &at);
                        try imports.append(gpa, .{ .module = module, .name = name });
                    },
                    .table => {
                        _ = try readByte(section, &at);
                        try skipLimits(section, &at);
                    },
                    .memory => try skipLimits(section, &at),
                    .global => {
                        _ = try readByte(section, &at);
                        _ = try readByte(section, &at);
                    },
                    .tag => {
                        _ = try readByte(section, &at);
                        _ = try readUnsigned(section, &at);
                    },
                    _ => return error.Truncated,
                }
            }
        }
        cursor = section_end;
    }
    return imports.toOwnedSlice(gpa);
}

2.7 The bridge as JavaScript

The bridge is Zig, but a page runs it as JavaScript. The build compiles bridge.zig with Zig's C backend - Zig's compiler writing C instead of machine code - for the wasm32 target, and a converter, tools/c2js.zig, turns that C into the JavaScript the page loads. The page's runtime is thus written once, in Zig, and checked by Zig's compiler; the only JavaScript written by hand is in Zig strings, run by the bridge with new Function.

   bridge.zig  --zig build-obj -ofmt=c-->  bridge.c  --c2js-->  JavaScript in the page
                                                        functions -> JS functions
                                                        globals   -> bytes in one data image

The converter lays every global out in one data image: a block of bytes copied into the page's heap at start, where each global sits at a fixed offset. The C backend writes each global's initial value as a C initializer, and the converter must read it exactly, since a wrong byte there is wrong in every page. The forms it reads are those the backend writes:

ZigC, as the backend writes itin the data image
a byte array up to 4 095 bytesa string literal, {"\003\n..."}, a long one split into adjacent pieces "..." "..."the bytes, the pieces decoded and then joined, as C joins them
a byte array of 4 096 bytes or morea list of character constants, {{'\x76','\x61',...}}one byte per constant
an undefined pointer((T const *)0xaaaaaaaaul)the integer under the casts: an integer cast keeps the bits its type holds
INT32_C(-1)an integer macro around a constantthe constant, evaluated
a floatzig_make_f32(0x1p0, 0x3f800000)its bits, the second argument
a pointer to a global(&g)the global's offset in the image
a pointer to a function(&f)the function's index in the page's function table: the value &f has at run time, so a vtable in a constant calls the same function

The second form is how a long string reaches the page: the backend writes a byte array of 4 096 bytes or more as a list of character constants, and the converter puts the bytes into the image, where the bridge reads them as text.

The converter reads only what it models. Anything else - a constant outside its grammar, a field its layout of the struct lacks, an address inside a global, a struct whose layout it does not know - is an error naming the C line, and the page is not built. A guessed value, a zero standing for something not understood, would be silently wrong in every page built from it; an error is found at once, by whoever builds next. The converter is tested by compiling a set of Zig programs both natively and through C and the converter, and requiring both to compute the same result.

2.8 A program in an audio worklet

A browser plays sound on a thread of its own, the audio thread, and a page can run code there in an audio worklet: a small program the audio thread calls each time it needs the next 128 frames of sound. (What zimr's worklet does - it runs the mixer's renderer in a second instance of the app's module - is in the sound tutorial; this section is about running converted code there at all.) The audio thread must never wait - a block not ready in time is a gap in the sound - so the scope a worklet's program runs in is bare. It has the JavaScript language and almost nothing of the browser: no atob, no TextDecoder, no TextEncoder. And the program is loaded as a module, which runs in strict mode: JavaScript's stricter rules, under which, for one, a variable must be declared before it is assigned.

A converted program uses all three. Its data section - the Zig program's constant bytes - travels as base64 text, turned back into bytes when the program starts; and the names of the properties and methods it reaches are UTF-8 bytes in its memory, turned into JavaScript strings as it reaches them. So the converter's prelude carries its own three, used only where the built-ins are missing, and each gives exactly what the built-in gives.

Base64 writes bytes as text: three bytes, 24 bits, as four characters of 6 bits each, from an alphabet of 64 - A to Z for 0 to 25, a to z for 26 to 51, 0 to 9 for 52 to 61, '+' for 62 and '/' for 63. Decoding goes back, four characters at a time:

   characters      T        W        F        u          ("TWFu" is the base64 of "Man")
   6-bit values    19       22       5        46
   24 bits         010011   010110   000101   101110
   3 bytes         01001101   01100001   01101110      =   77 'M',  97 'a',  110 'n'

When the bytes are not a multiple of three, the text ends in '=' padding - "==" after a last group of one byte, "=" after one of two - and the padding makes no bytes.

UTF-8 writes each character's number, its code point (from U+0000 to U+10FFFF), as one to four bytes. The first byte says how many follow; each following byte, a continuation, begins with the bits 10 and carries six more bits of the code point:

first bytebytescode pointsbits of the code point
0xxxxxxx1U+0000 to U+007F7
110xxxxx2U+0080 to U+07FF5 + 6
1110xxxx3U+0800 to U+FFFF4 + 6 + 6
11110xxx4U+10000 to U+10FFFF3 + 6 + 6 + 6
   e-acute  U+00E9    00011 101001              ->  11000011 10101001                     C3 A9
   euro     U+20AC    0010 000010 101100        ->  11100010 10000010 10101100            E2 82 AC
   a smile  U+1F600   000 011111 011000 000000  ->  11110000 10011111 10011000 10000000   F0 9F 98 80

A JavaScript string is not UTF-8 but UTF-16: a code point below U+10000 is one 16-bit unit, and one above it is two, a surrogate pair. The code point's offset past U+10000 has 20 bits; the first unit is D800 plus the top ten of them, the second DC00 plus the bottom ten. Decoding builds each code point from its bytes, then writes it as one unit or as a pair; encoding joins a pair back into its code point first.

   U+1F600 - U+10000  =  F600  =  0000111101 1000000000
   high surrogate        D800 + 0000111101 (3D)   =  D83D
   low surrogate         DC00 + 1000000000 (200)  =  DE00

Some bytes are not UTF-8: a continuation where a first byte was expected, a sequence cut short, a code point written with more bytes than it needs, the number of a surrogate - those belong to UTF-16 only - or a number past U+10FFFF. The Encoding Standard says what a decoder makes of each, and the browser's TextDecoder follows it, so the fallback does too: each broken piece becomes one U+FFFD, the replacement character, and the byte that broke a sequence starts the next one. After four first bytes it narrows the range the next byte may take - after E0 and F0, which rules out the forms longer than needed; after ED, the surrogates; after F4, the numbers past U+10FFFF - so that a single comparison rejects each of them.

A class. A worklet's program must also give the browser a class: it calls registerProcessor(name, SomeClass), where SomeClass extends the browser's AudioWorkletProcessor. A class is a function that makes objects, and the objects take their methods from the class's prototype, an object all of them share. A class that extends another must call the other's constructor - super(...) - before anything else: the other class sets the object up first, and AudioWorkletProcessor's constructor is where a processor receives its message port, its line to the main thread. Zig has no way to write a class, so the converter's runtime has one function that writes one, js_subclass, and the program sets the class's methods on its prototype itself:

   Zig                                          the JavaScript it amounts to
   const Processor = js_subclass(Base, init);   class Processor extends Base {
                                                  constructor(...a) { super(...a); init(this, ...a); }
                                                }
   js_set(Processor.prototype, "process", f);   Processor.prototype.process = f;

With the three fallbacks and the class, a program written in Zig and converted can run in an audio worklet as it does on a page.

3. Objects and lifetimes

3.1 Handles

The engine never holds a GPU object, only a handle to one. On the web, a handle indexes the bridge's table of JavaScript objects. In the Vulkan host it names a slot in a pool: an array of slots, one per live object, each holding the Vulkan handles and whatever else the host keeps about the object - a buffer's memory, a texture's format and size. A slot freed by a destroy is reused by the next object of its kind.

Reuse creates a hazard. A handle kept by mistake after its object was destroyed would, once the slot is reused, silently name a different object. So each slot carries a generation, a counter that changes every time the slot is freed, and the handle carries the generation it was issued with:

    31            24 23                                          0
   +----------------+---------------------------------------------+
   |   generation   |               slot index + 1                |
   +----------------+---------------------------------------------+

   0 is no object at all (slot index + 1 is never 0).
   A handle whose generation differs from its slot's is stale: it outlived its object.

In the code, the diagram is the type itself. A packed struct lays out its fields from the lowest bit up, so converting between a handle and its parts is a reinterpretation of the same 32 bits, with no shifts or masks to get wrong:

/// A handle as the engine holds it: which slot of its pool, and which generation of that slot. The slot is stored
/// plus one, so the all-zero handle names no object - zimr's and WebGPU's "none". Being a packed struct, its fields
/// ARE the bits: the slot in the low 24, the generation in the high 8.
const Handle = packed struct(u32) {
    slot_plus_one: u24,
    generation: u8,

    fn ofSlot(slot: u32, generation: u8) Handle {
        return .{ .slot_plus_one = @intCast(slot + 1), .generation = generation };
    }
};

Looking a handle up compares the two generations, and a stale handle is reported with the kind of object it was meant to name, instead of reaching the object that replaced it:

Pool
/// A table of host objects addressed by `Handle`s. Removing an object frees its slot for reuse under the next
/// generation, so a handle kept past its object's destruction no longer matches its slot, and `get` refuses it by
/// name instead of reaching the object that replaced it.
fn Pool(comptime Slot: type) type {
    return struct {
        entries: ArrayList(Entry) = .empty,
        free_slots: ArrayList(u32) = .empty,

        const Self = @This();
        const Entry = struct { slot: Slot, generation: u8, live: bool };
        /// The most slots a pool holds: as many as `slot_plus_one`'s 24 bits can number.
        const max_slots: u32 = zm.maxInt(u24);

        fn add(pool: *Self, gpa: Allocator, slot: Slot) Allocator.Error!u32 {
            if (pool.free_slots.pop()) |index| {
                const entry: *Entry = &pool.entries.items[index];
                entry.* = .{ .slot = slot, .generation = entry.generation +% 1, .live = true };
                return @bitCast(Handle.ofSlot(index, entry.generation));
            }
            const index: u32 = @intCast(pool.entries.items.len);
            if (index >= max_slots) {
                panic("vulkan host: more than {d} live objects of one kind", .{max_slots});
            }
            try pool.entries.append(gpa, .{ .slot = slot, .generation = 0, .live = true });
            return @bitCast(Handle.ofSlot(index, 0));
        }

        fn get(pool: *Self, handle: u32, comptime kind: []const u8) *Slot {
            const entry: *Entry = pool.entryOf(handle, kind);
            return &entry.slot;
        }

        /// The slot `handle` names, or null when its object was destroyed (or the handle names nothing): for an
        /// object that may legitimately be gone, where `get` would end the program.
        fn find(pool: *Self, handle: u32) ?*Slot {
            const entry: *Entry = pool.entryAt(handle) orelse return null;
            return if (holds(entry, handle)) &entry.slot else null;
        }

        fn remove(
            pool: *Self,
            gpa: Allocator,
            handle: u32,
            comptime kind: []const u8,
        ) Allocator.Error!Slot {
            const entry: *Entry = pool.entryOf(handle, kind);
            entry.live = false;
            const parts: Handle = @bitCast(handle);
            try pool.free_slots.append(gpa, @as(u32, parts.slot_plus_one) - 1);
            return entry.slot;
        }

        /// The entry the slot part of `handle` names, or null when it names none (handle 0, or past the table).
        fn entryAt(pool: *Self, handle: u32) ?*Entry {
            const parts: Handle = @bitCast(handle);
            const is_null: bool = parts.slot_plus_one == 0;
            const is_past_table: bool = parts.slot_plus_one > pool.entries.items.len;
            if (is_null or is_past_table) {
                return null;
            }
            return &pool.entries.items[parts.slot_plus_one - 1];
        }

        /// Whether `entry` still holds the object `handle` was given for: live, and not reused since (a reused slot
        /// has a newer generation).
        fn holds(entry: *const Entry, handle: u32) bool {
            const parts: Handle = @bitCast(handle);
            return entry.live and entry.generation == parts.generation;
        }

        fn entryOf(pool: *Self, handle: u32, comptime kind: []const u8) *Entry {
            const entry: *Entry = pool.entryAt(handle) orelse
                panic("vulkan host: unknown {s} handle {d}", .{ kind, handle });
            if (!holds(entry, handle)) {
                panic("vulkan host: {s} handle {d} used after its object was destroyed", .{ kind, handle });
            }
            return entry;
        }

        fn deinit(pool: *Self, gpa: Allocator) void {
            pool.entries.deinit(gpa);
            pool.free_slots.deinit(gpa);
        }
    };
}

3.2 The browser's object table

On the web, destroying an object is WebGPU's own operation, and WebGPU allows it at any time: the browser marks the object destroyed, refuses it in later commands, and keeps it alive until the GPU has finished the commands already submitted. The bridge only has to tell WebGPU, and forget the handle:

jsBufferDestroy
fn jsBufferDestroy(id: f64) void {
    const buf: Value = tblGet(id);
    if (!buf.isNull()) {
        _ = buf.call("destroy", .{});
    }
    tblRelease(id);
}
tblRelease
fn tblRelease(id: f64) void {
    _ = g.wgpu.objects.call("delete", .{Value{ .h = js_num(id) }});
}

3.3 Deferred destruction

Vulkan gives no such guarantee. The GPU executes commands some time after the program submits them - often a frame or two later - and destroying a buffer while a submitted command still reads it is an error with undefined consequences. The program must know when the GPU is done with each object.

The Vulkan host knows it through a timeline semaphore: a 64-bit counter that the GPU advances as work completes. Every submission is numbered - 1, 2, 3 and on - and asks the GPU to set the counter to its number when it finishes. Reading the counter then tells the program, without waiting, which submissions are complete. A released object is not destroyed at once; it is queued with the number of the last submission that may use it, and destroyed when the counter passes that number:

   CPU   submit #41       submit #42             buffer B released        submit #43
           |                 |                    -> queued "after #42"      |
           v                 v                                               v
   GPU   [ #41 executes ][ #42 executes, reads B ][ #43 executes ]
                                                 ^
                              the counter reaches 42: B is destroyed here, and not before

What waits in the queue is a small description of each Vulkan object to destroy:

Destroy
/// A Vulkan object the engine has released but the GPU may still be using.
const Destroy = union(enum) {
    buffer: struct { buffer: vk.Buffer, memory: vk.DeviceMemory },
    image: struct { image: vk.Image, memory: vk.DeviceMemory },
    image_view: vk.ImageView,
    sampler: vk.Sampler,
    descriptor_set: DescriptorSetSlot,
    descriptor_set_layout: vk.DescriptorSetLayout,
    pipeline_layout: vk.PipelineLayout,
    pipeline: vk.Pipeline,
};

Releasing an object queues it with the right number:

/// Releases a Vulkan object once nothing can still use it. WebGPU lets a program release an object the GPU may still be
/// using; Vulkan requires the program to wait. So the object is numbered with the submission that could last use it
/// - the next one while commands are unsubmitted (an open encoder, or pending writes), else the last one - and
/// destroyed when the timeline passes that number (`retireDestroys`).
fn deferDestroy(self: *Host, destroy: Destroy) Allocator.Error!void {
    // Commands not yet submitted - an open encoder's, or the write calls' in `uploads` (a buffer created, written and
    // released before any submit) - may use the object: it waits for the number of the submit that carries them.
    if (self.open_encoders > 0 or self.uploads != .null_handle) {
        try self.pending_destroys.append(self.gpa, destroy);
    } else {
        try self.retiring_destroys.append(self.gpa, .{ .serial = self.submitted, .destroy = destroy });
    }
}

and after every submission - and before each frame's work begins - the host reads the counter and destroys everything whose submission has completed. Reclaiming at submission matters for a host without a window: it never waits for a frame, so the submissions are its only regular moments:

retireCompleted
/// Reads how far the GPU has come, and reclaims everything it has finished with. Each list is compacted in place:
/// finished entries are acted on, and the others move down, keeping their submission order.
fn retireCompleted(self: *Host) BringUpError!void {
    _ = try readCompleted(self);
    try retireCommandBuffers(self);
    try retireStagingChunks(self);
    retireDestroys(self);
}
retireDestroys
/// Destroys each released object whose submission has finished; the others keep their order.
fn retireDestroys(self: *Host) void {
    var kept_destroys: usize = 0;
    for (self.retiring_destroys.items) |retiring| {
        if (retiring.serial <= self.completed) {
            executeDestroy(self, retiring.destroy);
        } else {
            self.retiring_destroys.items[kept_destroys] = retiring;
            kept_destroys += 1;
        }
    }
    self.retiring_destroys.shrinkRetainingCapacity(kept_destroys);
}
executeDestroy
/// Destroys one released object, now that the GPU has finished every submission that could use it. A buffer or an
/// image also frees its memory allocation (each has its own), lowering the count `allocateMemory` checks; a bind
/// group's descriptor set goes back to its pool page, which then has room again.
fn executeDestroy(self: *Host, destroy: Destroy) void {
    switch (destroy) {
        .buffer => |buffer| {
            self.device_dispatch.destroyBuffer(self.device, buffer.buffer, null);
            self.device_dispatch.freeMemory(self.device, buffer.memory, null);
            self.allocations -= 1;
        },
        .image => |image| {
            self.device_dispatch.destroyImage(self.device, image.image, null);
            self.device_dispatch.freeMemory(self.device, image.memory, null);
            self.allocations -= 1;
        },
        .image_view => |view| self.device_dispatch.destroyImageView(self.device, view, null),
        .sampler => |sampler| self.device_dispatch.destroySampler(self.device, sampler, null),
        .descriptor_set => |group| {
            const page: *DescriptorPage = &self.descriptor_pages.items[group.page];
            const sets = [_]vk.DescriptorSet{group.set};
            _ = self.device_dispatch.freeDescriptorSets(self.device, page.pool, 1, &sets);
            page.has_room = true;
        },
        .descriptor_set_layout => |layout| self.device_dispatch.destroyDescriptorSetLayout(self.device, layout, null),
        .pipeline_layout => |layout| self.device_dispatch.destroyPipelineLayout(self.device, layout, null),
        .pipeline => |pipeline| self.device_dispatch.destroyPipeline(self.device, pipeline, null),
    }
}

3.4 Unsubmitted commands

A submission number exists only once commands are submitted. Commands still being recorded have none yet, and they may use the object being released: an open command encoder may have bound the buffer, and the host's own upload commands - recorded by write calls, and sent with the next submission - may copy into it. While either kind of recording is in progress, a released object waits in a pending list instead, and receives the number of the submission that finally carries those commands. The first branch of deferDestroy above is this rule.

3.5 Null handles

Two edge cases complete the picture. Releasing handle 0 - an object that was never created - does nothing, exactly as the browser's table does nothing when asked to forget a missing entry. Releasing a stale handle is an error, reported by name: the browser would also ignore it, but a handle released twice is a mistake in the program, and the native host is the place where it becomes visible.

/// Releases a buffer; it is destroyed once the GPU has finished with it.
pub fn bufferDestroy(buffer: u32) void {
    // Releasing nothing (handle 0) is a no-op, as the browser's object table makes it.
    if (buffer == 0) {
        return;
    }
    const self: *Host = theHost();
    const slot: BufferSlot = self.buffers.remove(self.gpa, buffer, "buffer") catch outOfMemory();
    deferDestroy(self, .{ .buffer = .{ .buffer = slot.buffer, .memory = slot.memory } }) catch outOfMemory();
}

3.6 Objects that hold others

WebGPU has two ways to let go of an object. Buffers and textures can be destroyed explicitly: after destroy(), using them is an error, in the browser too. Every other object is only released - the app drops its handle (section 3.2) - and lives on while something holds it. A pipeline holds the pipeline layout it was made from; a bind group holds the texture views and samplers it binds. An app may release a layout as soon as its pipelines exist, and a view as soon as its bind group does, and both go on working.

Vulkan does not hold for the app. Its binding calls use the pipeline layout itself for as long as commands are recorded with the pipeline, and a descriptor set refers to its image views and samplers by their Vulkan handles. So the host counts, for each object WebGPU would keep alive, the objects holding it, and remembers whether the app has released its handle. The object is destroyed - after the GPU is done with it, as every object is (section 3.3) - once both are gone:

ObjectHeld byWithout the count
Pipeline layoutEach pipeline made from itThe next draw or dispatch binds its groups against a destroyed layout
Texture viewEach bind group binding itA bound group points at a destroyed view
SamplerEach bind group binding itA bound group points at a destroyed sampler

Shader modules and bind group layouts need no count. Vulkan does not use a shader module once the pipelines made from it exist. A pipeline layout, by default, is valid only while the bind group layouts it was made from are - but Vulkan 1.3's maintenance4 feature, which the host enables, lifts that, so bind group layouts too are destroyed on release. Buffers and textures need no count either: they are destroyed explicitly, after which WebGPU, too, refuses their use.

The engine's compute host shows the pattern. For each kernel it makes a bind group layout, a pipeline layout, a shader module, the pipeline and the bind group - then releases the first three at once:

   step                               pipeline layout          held by         destroyed
   --------------------------------   ----------------------   -------------   ---------
   create the pipeline layout         app's handle             -               no
   create the pipeline from it        app's handle             1 pipeline      no
   release the pipeline layout        released                 1 pipeline      no
   dispatch: groups bound against it  released                 1 pipeline      no
   release the pipeline               released                 none            yes, once
                                                                               the GPU is done
/// Drops one reference to a pipeline layout, and destroys the layout once the app has released it and no pipeline
/// made from it remains.
fn releasePipelineLayout(self: *Host, handle: u32, holder: Holder) void {
    const slot: *PipelineLayoutSlot = self.pipeline_layouts.get(handle, "pipeline layout");
    switch (holder) {
        .app => slot.released = true,
        .dependent => slot.pipeline_count -= 1,
    }
    if (slot.released and slot.pipeline_count == 0) {
        const removed: PipelineLayoutSlot = self.pipeline_layouts.remove(self.gpa, handle, "pipeline layout") catch
            outOfMemory();
        deferDestroy(self, .{ .pipeline_layout = removed.layout }) catch outOfMemory();
    }
}

A released bind group gives up its views and samplers at once. Their destruction is numbered by the same rule as the group's own descriptor set (section 3.3), so neither outlives the other's last use.

4. Descriptor encoding

4.1 The wasm boundary

Most calls take a handful of numbers. Three descriptions do not fit in an argument list: a bind-group layout and a bind group are lists of entries of varying kinds, and a render pipeline gathers vertex layouts, entry-point names and a dozen states. In a browser, only numbers cross the boundary between WebAssembly and JavaScript, so these three are written as bytes into the module's memory and passed as a pointer and a length.

The same bytes then serve both hosts. The bridge reads them with a JavaScript DataView and builds WebGPU's descriptor objects; the Vulkan host reads them with the decoders in gpu_abi.zig and builds Vulkan's create-info structures. One encoding, written in one place, read in two:

                                            +--> bridge.zig: DataView reads --> a WebGPU descriptor
   gpu.zig encodes --> bytes in memory -----+
                       (pointer, length)    +--> gpu_abi.zig decodes ------> Vulkan create-infos

4.2 Bind-group layout encoding

Every number in a blob is little-endian and written without padding. A bind-group layout is an entry count followed by five 32-bit numbers per entry: the binding number, the stages that see it, the kind of binding, one number whose meaning depends on the kind, and the texture view's dimension. A layout with one uniform buffer at binding 0, seen by the vertex stage:

   offset   bytes          meaning
     0      01 00 00 00    1 entry
     4      00 00 00 00    binding 0
     8      01 00 00 00    visibility: the vertex stage (bit 0)
    12      00 00 00 00    kind: uniform buffer
    16      00 00 00 00    extra: a buffer's minimum binding size; 0 means any
    20      00 00 00 00    view dimension: not a texture

The engine writes it from the layout's entries:

encodeBindGroupLayoutEntries
pub fn encodeBindGroupLayoutEntries(
    gpa: Allocator,
    entries: []const shader_introspect.BindGroupLayoutEntry,
) ![]u8 {
    var buf: ArrayList(u8) = .empty;
    errdefer buf.deinit(gpa);

    try writeU32(gpa, &buf, @intCast(entries.len));
    for (entries) |e| {
        try writeU32(gpa, &buf, e.binding);
        try writeU32(gpa, &buf, @bitCast(e.visibility));
        const tag: u32 = switch (e.resource) {
            .uniform_buffer => 0,
            .storage_buffer => |sb| @as(u32, if (sb.read_only) 1 else 2),
            .sampler => 3,
            .texture => 4,
            .storage_texture => 5,
        };
        try writeU32(gpa, &buf, tag);
        const extra: u32 = switch (e.resource) {
            .uniform_buffer => |ub| @intCast(ub.min_size),
            .storage_buffer => |sb| @intCast(sb.min_size),
            .sampler => |s| @as(u32, if (s.filtering) 0 else 1),
            .texture => 0,
            .storage_texture => 0,
        };
        try writeU32(gpa, &buf, extra);
        const view_dim: u32 = switch (e.resource) {
            .texture => |t| @backingInt(t.view_dimension),
            .storage_texture => |st| @backingInt(st.view_dimension),
            else => 0,
        };
        try writeU32(gpa, &buf, view_dim);
    }
    return buf.toOwnedSlice(gpa);
}

4.3 Decoding

Because nothing is padded, a 64-bit number - a bind group's buffer offset, say - can begin at any byte, and reading it as an aligned word would be wrong on some processors. The decoders therefore read bytes, never words, through one small reader that also refuses to run past the end of the blob:

BlobReader
/// Reads a blob front to back: little-endian numbers at any byte offset.
pub const BlobReader = struct {
    bytes: []const u8,
    position: usize = 0,

    fn take(reader: *BlobReader, count: usize) DecodeError![]const u8 {
        if (reader.bytes.len - reader.position < count) {
            return DecodeError.BlobTruncated;
        }
        const taken: []const u8 = reader.bytes[reader.position .. reader.position + count];
        reader.position += count;
        return taken;
    }

    pub fn readU32(reader: *BlobReader) DecodeError!u32 {
        const bytes: []const u8 = try reader.take(4);
        return readInt(u32, bytes[0..4], .little);
    }

    pub fn readU64(reader: *BlobReader) DecodeError!u64 {
        const bytes: []const u8 = try reader.take(8);
        return readInt(u64, bytes[0..8], .little);
    }

    pub fn readF64(reader: *BlobReader) DecodeError!f64 {
        return @bitCast(try reader.readU64());
    }

    /// A length-prefixed string, as a slice into the blob.
    pub fn readString(reader: *BlobReader) DecodeError![]const u8 {
        const length: u32 = try reader.readU32();
        return reader.take(length);
    }

    /// A u32 as a value of `Enumeration`; a number that names none is `BlobBadValue`.
    pub fn readEnum(reader: *BlobReader, comptime Enumeration: type) DecodeError!Enumeration {
        const raw: u32 = try reader.readU32();
        return fromInt(Enumeration, raw) orelse DecodeError.BlobBadValue;
    }
};

A decoder fills a caller's array with typed entries and allocates nothing, so the Vulkan host decodes into a fixed array on its stack:

BindGroupLayoutEntry
pub const BindGroupLayoutEntry = struct {
    binding: u32,
    visibility: ShaderStage,
    kind: BindingKind,
    /// Buffers: the minimum binding size (0: any). Samplers: 1 when the sampler must not filter.
    extra: u32,
    view_dimension: TextureViewDimension,
};
/// The entries of a bind-group layout blob, written into `entries` (which must have room for all of them).
pub fn decodeBindGroupLayout(blob: []const u8, entries: []BindGroupLayoutEntry) DecodeError![]BindGroupLayoutEntry {
    var reader: BlobReader = .{ .bytes = blob };
    const count: u32 = try reader.readU32();
    if (count > entries.len) {
        return DecodeError.BlobTooManyEntries;
    }
    for (entries[0..count]) |*entry| {
        const binding: u32 = try reader.readU32();
        const visibility: ShaderStage = @bitCast(try reader.readU32());
        const kind: BindingKind = try reader.readEnum(BindingKind);
        const extra: u32 = try reader.readU32();
        const view_dimension: TextureViewDimension = try reader.readEnum(TextureViewDimension);
        entry.* = .{
            .binding = binding,
            .visibility = visibility,
            .kind = kind,
            .extra = extra,
            .view_dimension = view_dimension,
        };
    }
    return entries[0..count];
}

4.4 Shared encoding

A format with one writer and two readers drifts as soon as a field is added on one side only. Each encoder in gpu.zig is therefore paired with its decoder in a test that encodes with the engine's real encoder and decodes with the Vulkan host's decoder; a field changed on one side and not the other fails it:

Encoding a bind group and decoding it back
test "gpu_abi round trip: a bind group's buffer range, sampler and view decode to what was encoded" {
    const gpa: Allocator = testing_allocator;
    const entries = [_]BindGroupEntry{
        .{
            .binding = 0,
            .resource = .{ .buffer = .{ .handle = @fromBackingInt(@intCast(5)), .offset = 256, .size = 1024 } },
        },
        .{ .binding = 1, .resource = .{ .sampler = @fromBackingInt(@intCast(6)) } },
        .{ .binding = 2, .resource = .{ .texture_view = @fromBackingInt(@intCast(7)) } },
    };
    const blob: []u8 = try encodeBindGroupEntries(gpa, &entries);
    defer gpa.free(blob);
    var room: [4]gpu_abi.BindGroupEntry = undefined;
    const decoded: []gpu_abi.BindGroupEntry = try gpu_abi.decodeBindGroup(blob, &room);
    try expectEqual(@as(usize, 3), decoded.len);
    try expectEqual(gpu_abi.BindingResource.buffer, decoded[0].resource);
    try expectEqual(@as(u32, 5), decoded[0].handle);
    try expectEqual(@as(u64, 256), decoded[0].offset);
    try expectEqual(@as(u64, 1024), decoded[0].size);
    try expectEqual(gpu_abi.BindingResource.sampler, decoded[1].resource);
    try expectEqual(@as(u32, 6), decoded[1].handle);
    try expectEqual(gpu_abi.BindingResource.texture_view, decoded[2].resource);
    try expectEqual(@as(u32, 7), decoded[2].handle);
}

5. Shaders

Each GPU API takes shaders in its own language: WebGPU takes WGSL, Vulkan takes SPIR-V, Metal takes the Metal Shading Language. zimr writes every engine shader once, in Zig, and derives the others from it at build time. This chapter follows one shader - the 2D renderer's fragment shader, which every 2D example runs - from its Zig source to each form a GPU receives.

5.1 Shaders in Zig

A shader has two parts. Its interface - what it receives from the previous stage, the textures it samples, what it outputs - is declared as Zig types in a companion file. Here the shader receives an interpolated texture coordinate and color, samples one texture, and outputs one color:

Samplers
/// Texture samplers.  Single sampler bound to slot 1 (.albedo).
/// Engine `loadShader` walks this struct at load time and binds the
/// texture+sampler to its declared slot via the material bind group.
///
/// The sampler slot/binding numbering: WebGL uses sequential ints
/// (0, 1, 2...); WebGPU uses an explicit `@group(N) @binding(N)` pair.
/// `Sampler2D(.albedo, .{})` maps to the engine's per-material bind group
/// - see `src/renderer_2d.zig::buildMaterialBindGroup` for the wgpu
/// side and the equivalent in `rlgl.zig` for the GL side.
pub const Samplers = struct {
    texture0: shader.Sampler2D(.albedo, .{}),
};
Outputs
/// Stage output: the final fragment color.  RGBA, sRGB pre-multiplied
/// or not depending on the swapchain configuration; the engine doesn't
/// gamma-correct here.
pub const Outputs = struct {
    out_color: Vec,
};

Its body is an ordinary Zig function from the interface's inputs to its outputs:

/// Pure-logic kernel.  Sample the bound texture at the interpolated
/// UV; multiply by the interpolated tint; that's the fragment colour.
/// Same shape as the legacy hand-written WGSL:
///   let sample = textureSample(t_albedo, s_albedo, in.uv);
///   return sample * in.color;
///
/// On SPIR-V the texture0 accessor is a native `OpSampledImage` +
/// `OpImageSampleImplicitLod` on the generated texture0 / texture0_sampler
/// handles.  On wgpu via spv2wgsl that translates to
/// `textureSample(texture0, texture0_sampler, uv)`
/// - handled by spv2wgsl's `emitImageSample`.  On CPU via
/// `raster_shader.dispatchFragmentShader` this calls
/// `io_in.texture0(uv)` which the raster pixel pipeline implements
/// via nearest-neighbour sampling of the bound texture's bytes.
pub fn shaderMain(io_in: Io) Out {
    var out: Out = undefined;
    const sample: Vec = io_in.texture0(io_in.frag_tex_coord);
    out.out_color = sample * io_in.frag_color;
    return out;
}

Because the body is plain Zig, the same function also runs on the processor: zimr's software rasterizer calls it for each pixel, which is how shaders are tested without a GPU. One definition serves the GPU through SPIR-V, the browser through WGSL, and the processor directly.

5.2 From Zig to SPIR-V

Zig's compiler can emit SPIR-V, the binary intermediate language that Vulkan consumes: a sequence of 32-bit words forming instructions - type declarations, variables decorated with their binding numbers and locations, functions, and an entry point that names the shader's stage. The build compiles each shader once, with zig build-obj -target spirv32-vulkan -ofmt=spirv, and the module it produces feeds both destinations:

   default_shapes_fs.zig + default_shapes_fs_io.zig
        |
        |  zig build-obj -target spirv32-vulkan -ofmt=spirv       (once, at build time)
        v
   SPIR-V: 32-bit words - types, decorated variables, one entry point
        |                                               |
        |  spv2wgsl (build time)                        |  embedded unchanged (native build)
        v                                               v
   WGSL, embedded in the WebAssembly              spv_vulkan.legalize (when the module is created)
        |                                               |
        v                                               v
   the browser compiles it                        vkCreateShaderModule

5.3 From SPIR-V to WGSL

WGSL is a text language with structured control flow - if, loop, switch - while SPIR-V expresses control flow as blocks and branches. spv2wgsl, zimr's translator, reads the module, rebuilds the structured statements from the branches, gives every value a name, and writes WGSL. Its entry point runs a fixed sequence of passes, and checks the result before returning it:

   SPIR-V words
     |  parse          types, constants, decorations, names, functions
     |  propagate      constant branch conditions folded, the blocks they make dead removed
     |  uniformity     a texture sampled outside uniform control flow is a build error
     |  interface      every input, output and binding must be decorated
     |  structurize    blocks and branches become if, loop and switch; every value gets a name
     |  check output   no non-finite constant; no texture or sampler held as a value;
     |                 every merge variable assigned on every path into it;
     |                 every storage buffer the SPIR-V uses still used by the WGSL
     v
   WGSL text

The entry point runs exactly that sequence, and each check's comment states the failure it rules out:

convertSpirvToWgslEntry
/// Entry-filtered translation: when `entry` is non-null, only the matching
/// OpEntryPoint is emitted (the multi-kernel path translates a module once
/// per kernel). See `State.wanted_entry`.
pub fn convertSpirvToWgslEntry(
    arena: Allocator,
    spirv: []const u32,
    entry: ?[]const u8,
) ![]const u8 {
    // SCCP prepass: fold spurious constant guards (e.g. the always-true
    // post-loop `if (phi == K)` Zig's un-optimized SPIR-V leaves), drop the
    // now-dead blocks, prune dead OpPhi operands - so the structurizer sees
    // clean control flow and post-loop texture samples sit at uniform scope.
    // Any rewrite error falls back to the raw module, so folding can only
    // help: at worst, nothing is folded.
    const folded_spirv: []const u32 = sccp.rewrite(arena, spirv) catch spirv;

    // THE SAMPLER-UNIFORMITY GATE. Runs on the FOLDED module, so the dead
    // `if (73u == 73u)` phi-dispatch guards SCCP already resolved cannot cause a
    // bogus report. This turns a device-only, pipeline-creation failure ("'textureSample'
    // must only be called from uniform control flow") into a BUILD failure with the
    // shader named. It is the only guard that can see this class: the branch is
    // usually manufactured inside a zm helper and exists nowhere in the Zig source.
    try sampler_uniformity.check(arena, folded_spirv, entry orelse "shader");

    var state = try State.init(arena, folded_spirv);
    state.wanted_entry = entry;

    try pass1_walk(&state);
    try pass2_decorations(&state);
    // Decorations and names are known from here on - fail a graphics shader with an
    // undecorated interface variable before anything gets auto-numbered.
    try checkGraphicsInterfaceDecorated(&state);
    try markAtomicBindings(&state);
    try checkBarrierUniformity(&state);
    try pass3_types_globals(&state);
    try pass4_functions(&state);

    var final: ArrayList(u8) = .empty;
    try final.appendSlice(arena, state.header_buf.items);
    try final.appendSlice(arena, state.body_buf.items);
    const out: []const u8 = try final.toOwnedSlice(arena);

    // ---- HARD CHECKS: OUTPUT NO BROWSER COMPILES ----
    //
    // A non-finite constant (a NaN or an infinity literal) and a texture or sampler held as an
    // ordinary value are WGSL that browsers refuse - and the refusal comes at pipeline creation,
    // on the device, naming no cause. There is no use for WGSL that cannot compile, so both fail
    // the build, in every mode, with the shader named. (`checkOutputClosure`, below, only warns.)
    try checkNonFiniteConstants(out);
    try checkNoHandleValues(out);

    // ---- GUARD: EVERY MERGE VARIABLE IS ASSIGNED ----
    //
    // A phi is how the structurizer carries a value across a control-flow join: a `var phiN`,
    // assigned on the paths into the join and read after it. WGSL zero-initialises a `var`, so a
    // phi that is declared and read but never assigned still compiles - and silently reads 0.
    // Every branch that tests it then takes the wrong arm, and a `while` loop runs its body exactly
    // once. Kernels without loops are untouched by the fault, so a test of only those cannot see
    // it; this check can.
    //
    // The invariant is purely syntactic, needs no GPU, and costs one pass over the output. It is
    // checked in every mode, not only Debug: a silently wrong shader is worse than a failed build,
    // and unlike the `_N` closure check below, no known limitation can trip it.
    try checkPhiClosure(arena, out);

    // ---- GUARD: EVERY STORAGE BUFFER THE SPIR-V USES IS STILL USED ----
    //
    // `checkPhiClosure` proves no phi is left unassigned; it cannot prove that no BLOCK was
    // dropped. A block can be: SCCP folds a selection whose condition is constant, and when the
    // merge carries a phi, a fold that deletes the wrong arm takes everything that arm dominated
    // with it - a whole loop, say - while the condition is still computed and the output stays
    // well formed:
    //
    //     let _4940: bool = phi4936 == 138u;   // computed...
    //     let _5545: u32 = phi4936;            // ...and never used: its `if` is gone
    //
    // A dropped block takes its memory operations with it. So if a function reads or writes
    // `kbuf_starts` in the SPIR-V, the WGSL emitted for it must read or write `kbuf_starts` too:
    // a buffer that stops appearing means code vanished. Cheap to notice, and no GPU needed.
    //
    // It is a MEMBERSHIP test, not a count: emission legitimately merges accesses (common
    // subexpressions, hoisting), so requiring the same NUMBER would fire on healthy shaders.
    //
    // It compares with the ORIGINAL SPIR-V, not the folded one: the fold is what deletes the
    // block, so the folded module lacks the dropped buffer too, and comparing it with the WGSL
    // would find nothing. Against the original, a correct fold still passes - SCCP removes only
    // provably dead arms, and a kernel touches its buffers on its live path, so no buffer loses
    // its last access to a correct fold.
    try checkBufferAccessSurvival(arena, spirv, out, entry orelse "shader");

    // ---- DIAGNOSTIC: EVERY `_N` THE OUTPUT REFERENCES IS DECLARED ----
    //
    // An opcode handler that forgets to name its result (`ids[N].wgsl_name`) leaves an undeclared
    // `_N` behind. This check reports it, in Debug builds, and returns the WGSL anyway: an opcode
    // the translator does not handle registers its result id as a bare name (the lazy placeholder
    // in `lookupId`), so an undeclared `_N` downstream of an emitted `// ERROR:` marker is
    // expected, and the marker is the real diagnostic. `checkOutputClosure` itself returns the
    // error, for callers (tests) that want the strict behaviour.
    if (builtin.mode == .debug) {
        var missing: u32 = 0;
        checkOutputClosure(arena, out, &missing) catch {
            // An undeclared `_N` DOWNSTREAM of an emitted `// ERROR:`
            // marker is a known, documented limitation (e.g. a GLSL
            // combined sampler - WGSL needs separate texture+sampler
            // bindings), not a forgotten `wgsl_name`: log it at `warn`
            // (informational; the marker is the real diagnostic).  A
            // missing declaration with NO error marker is the genuine
            // bug class this guard exists to catch - log at `err`.  The
            // WGSL is returned either way; the browser's WGSL frontend
            // rejects it at pipeline creation if it actually matters.
            if (indexOf(u8, out, "// ERROR:") != null) {
                std.log.warn(
                    "spv2wgsl: output references _{d} with no declaration, " ++
                        "downstream of an emitted // ERROR: marker (known limitation).",
                    .{missing},
                );
            } else {
                std.log.err(
                    "spv2wgsl: output references _{d} but it was never declared. " ++
                        "An opcode handler likely failed to set ids[%{d}].wgsl_name.",
                    .{ missing, missing },
                );
            }
        };
    }

    return out;
}

The output checks are cheap text-level passes that need no GPU. Each guards a mistake that would otherwise compile in some browsers and run wrongly, or fail only on a device: a value the translator forgot to assign reads as zero, and a block lost while rebuilding control flow takes its memory accesses with it, which shows up as a storage buffer the WGSL no longer mentions.

Most SPIR-V instructions have a direct WGSL counterpart, but the two languages do not agree on everything a value may be. In SPIR-V, loading a texture from its variable produces an ordinary value. In WGSL, textures and samplers are handles: they may live only in module-scope variables and function parameters, and a let may hold only a value of a constructible type. A texture load therefore binds no name at all; every use refers to the module-scope variable directly:

fn emitLoad(
    s: *State,
    out: *ArrayList(u8),
    ops: []const u32,
) !void {
    const tid: u32 = ops[0];
    const result: u32 = ops[1];
    const ptr_info: *const IdInfo = lookupId(s, ops[2]);
    const ptr: []const u8 = ptr_info.wgsl_name;
    const t: *const IdInfo = lookupType(s, tid);
    // A texture or a sampler is a HANDLE, and WGSL lets no `let` and no function-scope `var` hold one:
    // the type of a `let` must be constructible or a pointer. Chrome accepts
    // `let _1: texture_2d<f32> = texture0;`; Safari follows the specification and rejects it
    // ("'texture_2d<f32>' cannot be used as the type of a 'let'"). So a handle load binds nothing: the
    // result id names the module-scope variable itself (or the indexed expression, for an array of
    // textures), and every use substitutes that name - `textureSample(texture0, texture0_sampler, uv)`.
    if (isHandleTypeId(s, tid)) {
        setId(s, result, .{ .kind = .value, .type_id = tid, .wgsl_name = ptr });
        return;
    }
    const name: []const u8 = try tempName(s.arena, result);
    setId(s, result, .{ .kind = .value, .type_id = tid, .wgsl_name = name });
    try bindLhs(out, s, result, name, t.wgsl_name);
    // Atomic taint: a plain load of an `atomic<u32>` element is illegal WGSL;
    // route it through atomicLoad(&expr).  ptr is an access path like
    // `kbuf_grid_counts[idx]`; its root var (extra_a) being tainted is the cue.
    if (ptr_info.extra_a != 0 and ptr_info.extra_a < s.atomic_var.len and s.atomic_var[ptr_info.extra_a]) {
        try bprint(out, s.arena, "atomicLoad(&{s});\n", .{ptr});
        return;
    }
    try bprint(out, s.arena, "{s};\n", .{ptr});
}

For the running example, the sample becomes a direct use of the two module-scope handles:

@group(1) @binding(0) var texture0: texture_2d<f32>;
@group(1) @binding(1) var texture0_sampler: sampler;
...
    let _271: vec4<f32> = textureSample(texture0, texture0_sampler, uv);

A text-level check then refuses any output that holds a handle in a let, a function's var, or a return value, so a later change to the translator cannot reintroduce it:

checkNoHandleValues
/// Refuse WGSL that treats a texture or sampler HANDLE as an ordinary value: returned from a
/// function (`-> texture_2d<f32>`), held in a function-scope `var`, or bound with `let`. WGSL allows a
/// handle only as a module-scope binding or a function parameter. The `let` case is the subtle one:
/// the specification requires a `let`'s type to be constructible or a pointer, which a handle is not.
/// Safari enforces it ("'texture_2d<f32>' cannot be used as the type of a 'let'"); Chrome and Firefox
/// accept `let _1: texture_2d<f32> = texture0;`. `emitLoad` binds no name for a handle, and this check
/// refuses any output that does, so the translator's WGSL is what the strictest browser accepts.
///
/// Such WGSL would come from a shader that CALLS a function returning an `@extern` handle at runtime
/// (e.g. `shader_builtins.texture2D(...)` inside `main`) instead of declaring the handle at file scope:
/// the SPIR-V is valid, the WGSL is not.
///
/// Text-level, like `checkNonFiniteConstants`, and it reads only the TYPE: the text after `->` of a
/// function signature, and after the `:` of an indented `var` or `let`. A local merely NAMED
/// `sampler_count` or `texture_uv` (spv2wgsl carries Zig locals' names over) is therefore not taken
/// for a handle. A module-scope binding is at column 0 and legal.
fn checkNoHandleValues(wgsl: []const u8) !void {
    var lines = splitScalar(u8, wgsl, '\n');
    while (lines.next()) |line| {
        const trimmed: []const u8 = std.mem.trimStart(u8, line, " ");
        const is_indented: bool = trimmed.len < line.len;
        const return_type: ?[]const u8 = returnTypeOf(line);
        const var_type: ?[]const u8 = if (is_indented) localVarTypeOf(trimmed) else null;
        const let_type: ?[]const u8 = if (is_indented) localLetTypeOf(trimmed) else null;
        const returns_handle: bool = if (return_type) |t| isHandleType(t) else false;
        const holds_handle: bool = if (var_type) |t| isHandleType(t) else false;
        const binds_handle: bool = if (let_type) |t| isHandleType(t) else false;
        if (returns_handle or holds_handle) {
            std.log.warn("spv2wgsl: texture/sampler handle used as a value: `{s}`. Declare the " ++
                "handle as a file-scope `@extern` instead of calling a function for it.", .{trimmed});
            return error.WgslHandleUsedAsValue;
        }
        if (binds_handle) {
            std.log.warn("spv2wgsl: texture/sampler handle bound with `let`: `{s}`. WGSL forbids it " ++
                "(Safari rejects it); this is a translator bug - see `emitLoad`.", .{trimmed});
            return error.WgslHandleUsedAsValue;
        }
    }
}

5.4 SPIR-V for Vulkan

Vulkan takes SPIR-V directly, but it has rules of its own - the Vulkan environment specification - that a module produced for a general SPIR-V target does not always meet. The native host makes a legal copy of each module when the engine creates it, with four adjustments, and refuses the few modules it cannot fix:

legalize
/// A copy of `words` that Vulkan accepts: `DepthReplacing` declared where `FragDepth` is written, vertex-stage
/// storage buffers decorated `NonWritable`, pointer-type `ArrayStride`s dropped when nothing uses them, the Zig
/// source language declared `Unknown` - and `Linkage`, or a struct holding a runtime array without `Block`, refused.
/// The copy is owned (free it with `gpa.free`) and 4-byte aligned, as `vkCreateShaderModule` requires (an
/// `@embedFile` is only byte aligned).
/// Legalizing a legal module returns an identical copy.
pub fn legalize(gpa: Allocator, words: []const u32) Error![]u32 {
    var module: Module = try .parse(gpa, words);
    defer module.deinit(gpa);
    if (module.hasCapability(.Linkage)) {
        return Error.LinkageCapability;
    }

    // 1. The execution modes to add: DepthReplacing for every fragment entry that writes FragDepth.
    var new_execution_modes: ArrayList(u32) = .empty;
    defer new_execution_modes.deinit(gpa);
    for (module.entry_points.items) |entry_point| {
        const needs_depth_replacing: bool = entry_point.execution_model == .Fragment and
            !entry_point.has_depth_replacing and writesFragDepth(module, entry_point);
        if (needs_depth_replacing) {
            try new_execution_modes.appendSlice(gpa, &.{
                instructionHead(.ExecutionMode, 3),
                entry_point.id,
                @backingInt(ExecutionMode.DepthReplacing),
            });
        }
    }

    // 2. The decorations to add: NonWritable on every vertex-stage storage buffer that is provably read-only. A
    // written one is refused; one whose pointer escapes into a function call is left undecorated - legal under the
    // device's vertex-stores feature, which the host enables - rather than guessed read-only.
    var new_decorations: ArrayList(u32) = .empty;
    defer new_decorations.deinit(gpa);
    var decorated: AutoHashMapUnmanaged(u32, void) = .empty;
    defer decorated.deinit(gpa);
    var uses: StorageUses = try storageUses(gpa, module);
    defer uses.deinit(gpa);
    for (module.entry_points.items) |entry_point| {
        if (entry_point.execution_model != .Vertex) {
            continue;
        }
        for (entry_point.interface) |global_id| {
            if (!isUndecoratedStorage(module, global_id) or decorated.contains(global_id)) {
                continue;
            }
            if (uses.written.contains(global_id)) {
                return Error.VertexStageStorageWrite;
            }
            if (uses.escaped.contains(global_id)) {
                continue;
            }
            try decorated.put(gpa, global_id, {});
            try new_decorations.appendSlice(gpa, &.{
                instructionHead(.Decorate, 3),
                global_id,
