fn formatInfo(format: gpu_abi.TextureFormat) FormatInfo {
    return switch (format) {
        .undefined_ => .{ .format = .undefined, .texel_bytes = 0, .is_depth = false },
        .rgba8_unorm => .{ .format = .r8g8b8a8_unorm, .texel_bytes = 4, .is_depth = false },
        .rgba8_unorm_srgb => .{ .format = .r8g8b8a8_srgb, .texel_bytes = 4, .is_depth = false },
        .bgra8_unorm => .{ .format = .b8g8r8a8_unorm, .texel_bytes = 4, .is_depth = false },
        .bgra8_unorm_srgb => .{ .format = .b8g8r8a8_srgb, .texel_bytes = 4, .is_depth = false },
        .rgba16_float => .{ .format = .r16g16b16a16_sfloat, .texel_bytes = 8, .is_depth = false },
        .rgba32_float => .{ .format = .r32g32b32a32_sfloat, .texel_bytes = 16, .is_depth = false },
        .r8_unorm => .{ .format = .r8_unorm, .texel_bytes = 1, .is_depth = false },
        .rg8_unorm => .{ .format = .r8g8_unorm, .texel_bytes = 2, .is_depth = false },
        .depth16_unorm => .{ .format = .d16_unorm, .texel_bytes = 2, .is_depth = true },
        .depth24_plus, .depth32_float => .{ .format = .d32_sfloat, .texel_bytes = 4, .is_depth = true },
    };
}

WebGPU's depth24plus promises only "at least 24 bits" of depth, and some GPUs have no 24-bit depth attachment; the host uses a 32-bit float format, as Dawn and mach also choose. Creating a texture then mirrors creating a buffer: the usage flags mapped, device-local memory, and two commands into uploads - the image's one layout change, from undefined to GENERAL (chapter 7), and a clear to zeros, through the color or depth clear its aspect calls for:

createTexture
/// A 2D texture in device memory, moved to GENERAL (every image stays there) and cleared to zero - WebGPU guarantees a
/// new texture reads as zeros, as it does for buffers. Every texture can be copied from and to; the rest of its usage
/// follows WebGPU's.
fn createTexture(self: *Host, request: TextureRequest) BringUpError!u32 {
    const label: []const u8 = request.label;
    const format: gpu_abi.TextureFormat = std.enums.fromInt(gpu_abi.TextureFormat, request.format) orelse
        panic("vulkan host: texture `{s}` has an unknown format {d}", .{ label, request.format });
    if (format == .undefined_) {
        panic("vulkan host: texture `{s}` has no format", .{label});
    }
    const usage: gpu_abi.TextureUsage = @bitCast(request.usage);
    const info: FormatInfo = formatInfo(format);
    const is_depth: bool = info.is_depth;
    const usage_flags: vk.ImageUsageFlags = .{
        .transfer_src = true,
        .transfer_dst = true,
        .sampled = usage.texture_binding,
        .storage = usage.storage_binding,
        .depth_stencil_attachment = usage.render_attachment and is_depth,
        .color_attachment = usage.render_attachment and !is_depth,
    };
    const samples: vk.SampleCountFlags = sampleCountFlags(request.sample_count) orelse
        panic("vulkan host: texture `{s}` asks for {d} samples", .{ label, request.sample_count });
    const mip_levels: u32 = @max(request.mip_level_count, 1);
    const array_layers: u32 = @max(request.array_layers, 1);
    const image_info: vk.ImageCreateInfo = .{
        .image_type = .@"2d",
        .format = info.format,
        .extent = .{ .width = request.width, .height = request.height, .depth = 1 },
        .mip_levels = mip_levels,
        .array_layers = array_layers,
        .samples = samples,
        // The GPU's own arrangement of the texels, which only copies cross; the contents start undefined.
        .tiling = .optimal,
        .usage = usage_flags,
        .sharing_mode = .exclusive,
        .initial_layout = .undefined,
    };
    var image: vk.Image = .null_handle;
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.createImage(self.device, &image_info, null, &image));
    var requirements: vk.MemoryRequirements = .{};
    self.device_dispatch.getImageMemoryRequirements(self.device, image, &requirements);
    const device_local: vk.MemoryPropertyFlags = .{ .device_local = true };
    const memory: vk.DeviceMemory = try allocateMemory(self, requirements, .{}, device_local, label);
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.bindImageMemory(self.device, image, memory, 0));

    const range: vk.ImageSubresourceRange = .{
        .aspect_mask = aspectOf(is_depth),
        .level_count = mip_levels,
        .layer_count = array_layers,
    };
    const uploads: vk.CommandBuffer = try uploadsCommandBuffer(self);
    imageBarrier(self, uploads, image, range, .undefined, .general);
    // Zeros, as WebGPU promises for a new texture - a depth texture too, a depth of 0. (A render pass that clears
    // depth sets 1.0, the far end of the range: that is the start of drawing, not the texture's creation.)
    if (is_depth) {
        const zero_depth: vk.ClearDepthStencilValue = .{ .depth = 0, .stencil = 0 };
        self.device_dispatch.cmdClearDepthStencilImage(uploads, image, .general, &zero_depth, 1, @ptrCast(&range));
    } else {
        const zero_color: vk.ClearColorValue = .{ .float_32 = @splat(0) };
        self.device_dispatch.cmdClearColorImage(uploads, image, .general, &zero_color, 1, @ptrCast(&range));
    }
    nameObject(self, .image, @backingInt(image), label);
    return self.textures.add(self.gpa, .{
        .image = image,
        .memory = memory,
        .format = info.format,
        .is_depth = is_depth,
        .extent = .{ .width = request.width, .height = request.height },
        .mip_levels = mip_levels,
        .array_layers = array_layers,
        .samples = samples,
        .texel_bytes = info.texel_bytes,
    });
}

A shader or a pass never sees a texture directly, only a view of it: which mip levels and layers, read as which dimension. WebGPU's default view covers everything - a 2D view for one layer, a 2D-array view for several. A view can also cover a range: a single mip level, which a mipmap generator renders into one level at a time; a number of layers read as a 2D array; or one layer read as an ordinary 2D texture. A render target is always one level of one layer, so drawing into each layer of an array takes a view of each. A view's size is its first level's, halved per level and never below one texel - 8, 4, 2 and 1 across an 8-texel texture's four levels - because a render pass takes its size from its first view:

createTextureView
/// A view of a texture over `range`. A view's extent is its base level's - halved per level, never below 1 - since a
/// render pass sizes itself from its first view.
fn createTextureView(
    self: *Host,
    texture_handle: u32,
    range: ViewRange,
) BringUpError!u32 {
    const texture: TextureSlot = self.textures.get(texture_handle, "texture").*;
    const mip_count: u32 = range.mip_count orelse texture.mip_levels -| range.base_mip;
    const base_layer: u32 = range.base_layer orelse 0;
    const layer_count: u32 = if (range.base_layer != null) 1 else range.layer_count orelse texture.array_layers;
    const fits: bool = mip_count > 0 and range.base_mip + mip_count <= texture.mip_levels and layer_count > 0 and
        base_layer + layer_count <= texture.array_layers;
    if (!fits) {
        const message: []const u8 = "vulkan host: a view of mip levels {d}..{d} and {d} layers, " ++
            "of a texture with {d} levels and {d} layers";
        panic(message, .{
            range.base_mip,
            range.base_mip + mip_count,
            base_layer + layer_count,
            texture.mip_levels,
            texture.array_layers,
        });
    }
    const is_array: bool = range.base_layer == null and (range.layer_count != null or texture.array_layers > 1);
    const view_info: vk.ImageViewCreateInfo = .{
        .image = texture.image,
        .view_type = if (is_array) .@"2d_array" else .@"2d",
        .format = texture.format,
        .subresource_range = .{
            .aspect_mask = aspectOf(texture.is_depth),
            .base_mip_level = range.base_mip,
            .level_count = mip_count,
            .base_array_layer = base_layer,
            .layer_count = layer_count,
        },
    };
    var view: vk.ImageView = .null_handle;
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.createImageView(self.device, &view_info, null, &view));
    const base_extent: vk.Extent2D = .{
        .width = @max(1, texture.extent.width >> @intCast(range.base_mip)),
        .height = @max(1, texture.extent.height >> @intCast(range.base_mip)),
    };
    return self.texture_views.add(self.gpa, .{
        .view = view,
        .image = texture.image,
        .format = texture.format,
        .extent = base_extent,
        .is_swapchain_image = false,
        .is_depth = texture.is_depth,
        .samples = texture.samples,
    });
}

A view of the whole chain lets the GPU choose the level as it samples. It measures how far the texture coordinates move from one pixel to the next, counted in texels of level 0. When a 256-texel image covers 64 pixels on screen, each pixel spans four texels; reading level 0 there would take one texel in four and miss the rest, which is what makes a distant texture shimmer as it moves. Level 2, 64 texels across, already holds the average of each 4x4 block of level 0, so its texels are pixel-sized and it is the one to read. The level is the base-2 logarithm of the span, log2(4) = 2. A sampler with nearest-mip filtering reads the closest level; one with linear mip filtering blends the two levels around a fractional value.

   size on screen    texels per pixel    level read
   256 px                   1            0   (256 x 256)
   128 px                   2            1   (128 x 128)
    64 px                   4            2   (64 x 64)
    32 px                   8            3   (32 x 32)

The texture_views example makes the choice visible. It paints each level of a chain in its own colour, through a view of that level wrapped as a render target, then draws the whole chain at several sizes through a nearest-mip sampler: each quad comes out in the colour of the level the GPU read.

paintChain
/// Paints every level of the chain, each through a view of that level: a checkerboard of eight squares a side in the
/// level's colour - or one square per texel, once a level is smaller than eight - and the level's number where it
/// fits. The levels never change, so they are painted once.
fn paintChain(f: *z.Frame, s: *State) void {
    for (s.level_views, 0..) |view, level| {
        const size: u32 = levelSize(level);
        const color: Color = level_colors[level];
        z.beginTextureMode(f.gl, targetOf(s.chain, view, size), shade(color, 0.55));
        const squares: u32 = @min(size, 8);
        const square: f32 = float(size) / float(squares);
        for (0..squares) |row| {
            for (0..squares) |column| {
                if ((row + column) % 2 != 0) {
                    continue;
                }
                const square_rect: z.Rectangle = .{
                    .x = float(column) * square,
                    .y = float(row) * square,
                    .width = square,
                    .height = square,
                };
                f.gl.rect(square_rect, .{ .color = color });
            }
        }
        if (size >= 32) {
            const label_size: f32 = float(size) * 0.4;
            const label_position: [2]f32 = .{ float(size) * 0.32, float(size) * 0.28 };
            f.gl.textf(label_position, "{d}", .{level}, .{ .size = label_size, .color = text_color, .font = &s.font });
        }
        z.endTextureMode(f.gl);
    }
    s.is_chain_painted = true;
}

8.7 Texture writes

A texture write is staged like a buffer write, then copied into the image with a buffer-to-image copy. One difference matters: WebGPU measures the data's row pitch in bytes, Vulkan in texels. The host divides by the format's texel size, and refuses a pitch that is not a whole number of texels:

/// Where a texture write lands: a rectangle of one mip level of layer 0, rows `bytes_per_row` apart in the data.
const TextureRegion = struct {
    x: u32,
    y: u32,
    width: u32,
    height: u32,
    bytes_per_row: u32,
    mip_level: u32,
};
writeTexture
/// Stages `data` and records its copy into the texture, in `uploads` - ordered like every other write.
fn writeTexture(
    self: *Host,
    texture_handle: u32,
    region: TextureRegion,
    data: []const u8,
) BringUpError!void {
    if (data.len == 0 or region.width == 0 or region.height == 0) {
        return;
    }
    const texture: TextureSlot = self.textures.get(texture_handle, "texture").*;
    if (region.bytes_per_row % texture.texel_bytes != 0) {
        panic("vulkan host: texture rows of {d} bytes are not whole {d}-byte texels", .{
            region.bytes_per_row,
            texture.texel_bytes,
        });
    }
    const staged: Staged = try stage(self, data);
    const uploads: vk.CommandBuffer = try uploadsCommandBuffer(self);
    fullBarrier(self, uploads);
    const copy: vk.BufferImageCopy = .{
        .buffer_offset = staged.offset,
        // Vulkan measures the data's rows in texels; 0 rows of height means "the copy's own height".
        .buffer_row_length = region.bytes_per_row / texture.texel_bytes,
        .buffer_image_height = 0,
        .image_subresource = .{
            .aspect_mask = aspectOf(texture.is_depth),
            .mip_level = region.mip_level,
            .base_array_layer = 0,
            .layer_count = 1,
        },
        .image_offset = .{ .x = @intCast(region.x), .y = @intCast(region.y), .z = 0 },
        .image_extent = .{ .width = region.width, .height = region.height, .depth = 1 },
    };
    self.device_dispatch.cmdCopyBufferToImage(uploads, staged.buffer, texture.image, .general, 1, @ptrCast(&copy));
}

8.8 Copies from textures

Copying a texture into a buffer is the way back from the GPU's images to the CPU: the bytes land in a buffer, and a read (section 8.5) brings them over. The copy lays the texture out row by row. WebGPU states the distance from one row's start to the next in bytes, and requires a multiple of 256; Vulkan states it in texels. The host divides one by the other - which is why the pitch must be a whole number of texels, as a multiple of 256 always is for the formats a copy allows.

A 4 by 4 texture of 4-byte texels, copied with WebGPU's 256-byte pitch:

   buffer byte    0 ......... 15   16 ........ 255
                  row 0: 4 texels  (not written)
                256 ........ 271   272 ....... 511
                  row 1            (not written)
                512 ........ 527   528 ....... 767
                  row 2            (not written)
                768 ........ 783
                  row 3            (the last row needs only its texels: 3 * 256 + 16 = 784 bytes in all)

   Vulkan's row length: 256 / 4 = 64 texels

Every image the host makes lives in the general layout, which a copy can read; a full barrier first orders the copy after whatever drew the texture.

/// Copies a texture's first `width` by `height` texels - mip level 0, layer 0, from the corner - into a buffer, each
/// row `bytes_per_row` apart, as WebGPU's `copyTextureToBuffer` does with those arguments. Vulkan counts a row's pitch
/// in texels, so the pitch must be a whole number of them (WebGPU's 256-byte multiple always is). Every image lives in
/// the GENERAL layout, which a copy reads from; a full barrier first orders it after the work that drew the texture.
pub fn encoderCopyTextureToBuffer(
    encoder: u32,
    texture_handle: u32,
    destination_handle: u32,
    bytes_per_row: u32,
    width: u32,
    height: u32,
) void {
    const self: *Host = theHost();
    const command_buffer: vk.CommandBuffer = self.encoders.get(encoder, "command encoder").command_buffer;
    const texture: TextureSlot = self.textures.get(texture_handle, "texture").*;
    const destination: vk.Buffer = self.buffers.get(destination_handle, "buffer").buffer;
    if (bytes_per_row % texture.texel_bytes != 0) {
        panic("vulkan host: a texture copy's rows are {d} bytes apart, not a whole number of {d}-byte texels", .{
            bytes_per_row,
            texture.texel_bytes,
        });
    }
    fullBarrier(self, command_buffer);
    const region: vk.BufferImageCopy = .{
        .buffer_offset = 0,
        .buffer_row_length = bytes_per_row / texture.texel_bytes,
        .buffer_image_height = 0,
        .image_subresource = .{
            .aspect_mask = aspectOf(texture.is_depth),
            .mip_level = 0,
            .base_array_layer = 0,
            .layer_count = 1,
        },
        .image_offset = .{ .x = 0, .y = 0, .z = 0 },
        .image_extent = .{ .width = width, .height = height, .depth = 1 },
    };
    const regions: *const [1]vk.BufferImageCopy = &region;
    self.device_dispatch.cmdCopyImageToBuffer(command_buffer, texture.image, .general, destination, 1, regions);
}

8.9 Samplers and shader modules

A sampler describes how a shader reads a texture: the filters for magnification, minification and between mip levels, and what happens past the edges. The host creates each exactly as the browser's bridge does, so a texture reads the same on both: the address mode applies to the u and v axes, w keeps WebGPU's default, and the level-of-detail range is WebGPU's default too:

createSampler
/// A sampler, made exactly as the browser's bridge makes it, so a texture reads the same on both hosts: the three
/// filters, the address mode on u and v (w keeps WebGPU's default, clamp to edge), and WebGPU's default
/// level-of-detail range, 0 to 32 - every mip level a texture can have.
fn createSampler(self: *Host, request: SamplerRequest) BringUpError!u32 {
    const address_mode: vk.SamplerAddressMode = samplerAddressMode(request.address_mode);
    const sampler_info: vk.SamplerCreateInfo = .{
        .mag_filter = if (request.magnify_linear) .linear else .nearest,
        .min_filter = if (request.minify_linear) .linear else .nearest,
        .mipmap_mode = if (request.mipmap_linear) .linear else .nearest,
        .address_mode_u = address_mode,
        .address_mode_v = address_mode,
        .address_mode_w = .clamp_to_edge,
        .min_lod = 0,
        .max_lod = 32,
    };
    var sampler: vk.Sampler = .null_handle;
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.createSampler(self.device, &sampler_info, null, &sampler));
    return self.samplers.add(self.gpa, .{ .sampler = sampler });
}

A shader module is the last resource the engine creates before its pipelines. Natively, the bytes the engine passes are the compiler's SPIR-V; the host copies them into aligned words, legalizes them (chapter 5), and creates the module. A module whose bytes are not SPIR-V - a shader hand-written in WGSL - has no native path, and is refused by name:

createShaderModule
/// A shader module from the compiler's SPIR-V - natively, the bytes embedded under the shader's name are SPIR-V -
/// legalized on the way for Vulkan's rules, which the compiler's output does not all meet (`spv_vulkan.legalize`,
/// whose header lists each adjustment). The embedded bytes are only byte-aligned, so they are copied into words first.
fn createShaderModule(
    self: *Host,
    bytes: []const u8,
    label: []const u8,
) BringUpError!u32 {
    const not_spirv: []const u8 = "vulkan host: shader `{s}` is not SPIR-V - " ++
        "a module written in WGSL has no native path";
    const is_spirv_sized: bool = bytes.len >= 20 and bytes.len % 4 == 0;
    if (!is_spirv_sized) {
        panic(not_spirv, .{label});
    }
    const words: []u32 = try self.gpa.alloc(u32, bytes.len / 4);
    defer self.gpa.free(words);
    @memcpy(sliceAsBytes(words), bytes);
    const legal: []u32 = spv_vulkan.legalize(self.gpa, words) catch |err| switch (err) {
        error.NotSpirv => panic(not_spirv, .{label}),
        else => panic("vulkan host: shader `{s}` cannot run on Vulkan: {s}", .{ label, @errorName(err) }),
    };
    defer self.gpa.free(legal);
    const module_info: vk.ShaderModuleCreateInfo = .{ .code_size = legal.len * 4, .p_code = legal.ptr };
    var module: vk.ShaderModule = .null_handle;
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.createShaderModule(self.device, &module_info, null, &module));
    nameObject(self, .shader_module, @backingInt(module), label);
    return self.shader_modules.add(self.gpa, .{ .module = module });
}

9. Bindings and pipelines

A draw needs two kinds of input besides its vertices. Its shaders read resources - uniforms, textures, samplers - and those must be handed over in a form the GPU can find. And the GPU must be told, in advance, everything about how the draw runs: which shaders, how vertices are laid out, whether depth is tested, how colors blend. The first is the job of bind groups; the second, of pipelines.

9.1 Resource bindings

A shader names each resource it reads by two numbers: a group and a binding within the group. The engine supplies the resources group by group. A bind-group layout describes one group's shape - which bindings it has, and what kind of resource each is - and a bind group fills that shape with actual resources. A layout is like a blank form: it says which fields exist and what each must hold; a bind group is the form filled in. Vulkan has the same two objects under other names:

   in the shader (WGSL)                            in Vulkan
   @group(1) @binding(0) var texture0 ...      ->  set 1, binding 0: a sampled image
   @group(1) @binding(1) var texture0_sampler  ->  set 1, binding 1: a sampler

   bind-group layout   (the blank form)        ->  descriptor-set layout
   bind group          (the form filled in)    ->  descriptor set
   pipeline layout     (the forms, in order)   ->  pipeline layout

Each kind of WebGPU binding becomes a kind of Vulkan descriptor - the GPU's record of where a resource is and how to read it. Both kinds of storage buffer become the same descriptor: "read-only" is a promise WebGPU checks, not a different kind of record:

/// A WebGPU binding as a Vulkan descriptor: both storage-buffer kinds are storage
/// buffers (read-only is a promise WebGPU checks, not a different descriptor), textures are sampled images.
fn descriptorType(kind: gpu_abi.BindingKind) vk.DescriptorType {
    return switch (kind) {
        .uniform_buffer => .uniform_buffer,
        .read_only_storage_buffer, .storage_buffer => .storage_buffer,
        .sampler => .sampler,
        .texture => .sampled_image,
        .storage_texture => .storage_image,
    };
}

A layout also says which shader stages see each binding. The host turns a layout blob (chapter 4) into a descriptor-set layout, and keeps the decoded entries, because filling a group later needs each binding's kind:

stageFlags
/// The shader stages that see a binding, as Vulkan's stage flags.
fn stageFlags(visibility: gpu_abi.ShaderStage) vk.ShaderStageFlags {
    return .{ .vertex = visibility.vertex, .fragment = visibility.fragment, .compute = visibility.compute };
}
createBindGroupLayout
/// A descriptor-set layout from a bind-group layout blob; the decoded entries are kept for the groups made from it.
/// An empty blob makes a layout with no bindings, which the engine's placeholder groups use.
fn createBindGroupLayout(
    self: *Host,
    blob: []const u8,
    label: []const u8,
) BringUpError!u32 {
    var room: [max_bindings_per_group]gpu_abi.BindGroupLayoutEntry = undefined;
    const entries: []gpu_abi.BindGroupLayoutEntry = gpu_abi.decodeBindGroupLayout(blob, &room) catch |err| {
        panic("vulkan host: bind-group layout `{s}`: {s}", .{ label, @errorName(err) });
    };
    var bindings: [max_bindings_per_group]vk.DescriptorSetLayoutBinding = undefined;
    for (entries, bindings[0..entries.len]) |entry, *binding| {
        binding.* = .{
            .binding = entry.binding,
            .descriptor_type = descriptorType(entry.kind),
            .descriptor_count = 1,
            .stage_flags = stageFlags(entry.visibility),
        };
    }
    const layout_info: vk.DescriptorSetLayoutCreateInfo = .{
        .binding_count = @intCast(entries.len),
        .p_bindings = &bindings,
    };
    var layout: vk.DescriptorSetLayout = .null_handle;
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.createDescriptorSetLayout(self.device, &layout_info, null, &layout));
    nameObject(self, .descriptor_set_layout, @backingInt(layout), label);
    const kept: []gpu_abi.BindGroupLayoutEntry = try self.gpa.dupe(gpu_abi.BindGroupLayoutEntry, entries);
    return self.bind_group_layouts.add(self.gpa, .{ .layout = layout, .entries = kept });
}

9.2 Descriptor sets

Descriptor sets do not come from the device directly but from a descriptor pool: a block of storage for a fixed number of sets. The host keeps a list of pool pages, each with room for a few hundred sets. A new bind group takes a set from the newest page with room; a destroyed one returns its set to its page, which then has room again; a new page is added only when every page is full. Without that reuse, a program that creates and destroys a bind group each frame would add a page every few hundred frames, forever:

/// A descriptor pool page. Sets are allocated from a page until Vulkan reports it full; a destroyed bind group's
/// set returns to its page, which then has room again. `has_room` records the last answer, so allocation skips
/// pages known to be full instead of asking Vulkan each time.
const DescriptorPage = struct {
    pool: vk.DescriptorPool,
    has_room: bool = true,
};
/// One descriptor set of `layout`: from the newest page with room - newest first, since older pages are the likelier
/// to be full - and from a new page only when every page is full.
fn allocateDescriptorSet(self: *Host, layout: vk.DescriptorSetLayout) BringUpError!DescriptorSetSlot {
    var index: usize = self.descriptor_pages.items.len;
    while (index > 0) {
        index -= 1;
        const page: *DescriptorPage = &self.descriptor_pages.items[index];
        if (!page.has_room) {
            continue;
        }
        if (try allocateFromPool(self, page.pool, layout)) |set| {
            return .{ .set = set, .page = @intCast(index) };
        }
        page.has_room = false;
    }
    const pool: vk.DescriptorPool = try createDescriptorPoolPage(self);
    const set: vk.DescriptorSet = try allocateFromPool(self, pool, layout) orelse
        panic("vulkan host: a new descriptor pool page cannot hold one set", .{});
    return .{ .set = set, .page = @intCast(self.descriptor_pages.items.len - 1) };
}

A WebGPU bind group never changes after it is created, so its set is written once, at creation: for each entry, the buffer range, the texture view or the sampler, checked against the binding the layout declares. A buffer size of 0 means "to the end of the buffer":

createBindGroup
/// A bind group: one descriptor set, written once - WebGPU bind groups never change after creation. Each entry's
/// descriptor type comes from its layout's binding; a buffer size of 0 binds the rest of the buffer.
fn createBindGroup(
    self: *Host,
    layout_handle: u32,
    blob: []const u8,
    label: []const u8,
) BringUpError!u32 {
    const layout: BindGroupLayoutSlot = self.bind_group_layouts.get(layout_handle, "bind-group layout").*;
    var room: [max_bindings_per_group]gpu_abi.BindGroupEntry = undefined;
    const entries: []gpu_abi.BindGroupEntry = gpu_abi.decodeBindGroup(blob, &room) catch |err| {
        panic("vulkan host: bind group `{s}`: {s}", .{ label, @errorName(err) });
    };
    var group: BindGroupSlot = .{ .descriptor_set = try allocateDescriptorSet(self, layout.layout) };
    var buffer_infos: [max_bindings_per_group]vk.DescriptorBufferInfo = undefined;
    var image_infos: [max_bindings_per_group]vk.DescriptorImageInfo = undefined;
    var writes: [max_bindings_per_group]vk.WriteDescriptorSet = undefined;
    for (entries, 0..) |entry, index| {
        const kind: gpu_abi.BindingKind = bindingKind(layout.entries, entry.binding, label);
        writes[index] = .{
            .dst_set = group.descriptor_set.set,
            .dst_binding = entry.binding,
            .descriptor_count = 1,
            .descriptor_type = descriptorType(kind),
        };
        switch (entry.resource) {
            .buffer => {
                const buffer: *BufferSlot = self.buffers.get(entry.handle, "buffer");
                buffer_infos[index] = .{
                    .buffer = buffer.buffer,
                    .offset = entry.offset,
                    .range = if (entry.size == 0) vk.whole_size else entry.size,
                };
                writes[index].p_buffer_info = @ptrCast(&buffer_infos[index]);
            },
            .sampler => {
                const sampler: *SamplerSlot = self.samplers.get(entry.handle, "sampler");
                image_infos[index] = .{ .sampler = sampler.sampler };
                writes[index].p_image_info = @ptrCast(&image_infos[index]);
                sampler.bind_group_count += 1;
                group.held[group.held_count] = .{ .kind = .sampler, .handle = entry.handle };
                group.held_count += 1;
            },
            .texture_view => {
                const view: *TextureViewSlot = self.texture_views.get(entry.handle, "texture view");
                image_infos[index] = .{ .image_view = view.view, .image_layout = .general };
                writes[index].p_image_info = @ptrCast(&image_infos[index]);
                view.bind_group_count += 1;
                group.held[group.held_count] = .{ .kind = .texture_view, .handle = entry.handle };
                group.held_count += 1;
            },
        }
    }
    const no_copies = [_]vk.CopyDescriptorSet{};
    self.device_dispatch.updateDescriptorSets(self.device, @intCast(entries.len), &writes, 0, &no_copies);
    nameObject(self, .descriptor_set, @backingInt(group.descriptor_set.set), label);
    return self.bind_groups.add(self.gpa, group);
}

9.3 Pipeline layouts

A pipeline layout lists the group layouts a pipeline uses, in order: group 0, group 1, group 2. WebGPU's group number is Vulkan's set number, unchanged. A group the pipeline does not use is an empty slot, filled with a layout that has no bindings at all:

createPipelineLayout
/// A pipeline layout over the groups' set layouts, without push constants. WebGPU's group number IS the Vulkan set
/// number (every device binds at least 4, checked at bring-up); a 0 handle - an empty slot - gets the empty layout.
fn createPipelineLayout(
    self: *Host,
    layout_handles: []const u32,
    label: []const u8,
) BringUpError!u32 {
    if (layout_handles.len > max_bind_groups) {
        panic("vulkan host: pipeline layout `{s}` has {d} groups; zimr binds at most {d}", .{
            label,
            layout_handles.len,
            max_bind_groups,
        });
    }
    var set_layouts: [max_bind_groups]vk.DescriptorSetLayout = undefined;
    var empty_sets: GroupSet = .empty;
    for (layout_handles, set_layouts[0..layout_handles.len], 0..) |handle, *set_layout, index| {
        if (handle == 0) {
            set_layout.* = try emptySetLayout(self);
            empty_sets.set(index);
            continue;
        }
        const group_layout: *BindGroupLayoutSlot = self.bind_group_layouts.get(handle, "bind-group layout");
        set_layout.* = group_layout.layout;
        if (group_layout.entries.len == 0) {
            empty_sets.set(index);
        }
    }
    const layout_info: vk.PipelineLayoutCreateInfo = .{
        .set_layout_count = @intCast(layout_handles.len),
        .p_set_layouts = &set_layouts,
    };
    var layout: vk.PipelineLayout = .null_handle;
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.createPipelineLayout(self.device, &layout_info, null, &layout));
    nameObject(self, .pipeline_layout, @backingInt(layout), label);
    return self.pipeline_layouts.add(self.gpa, .{
        .layout = layout,
        .set_count = @intCast(layout_handles.len),
        .empty_sets = empty_sets,
    });
}

9.4 Render pipeline state

A render pipeline fixes, in one object, everything about how a draw runs. It is easiest to understand as the path a triangle takes through the GPU, with the setting that controls each stop:

   vertex buffers
        |   vertex input: each buffer's stride; each attribute's format and location
        v
   vertex shader                      the module and entry point named in the pipeline
        |
        v
   primitive assembly                 the topology: points, lines, triangles, strips
        |
        v
   rasterizer                         which faces are culled; which winding is "front"
        |   one fragment per covered pixel
        v
   fragment shader
        |
        v
   depth test                         compare with the depth buffer; write it or not
        |
        v
   blending                           how the new color mixes with the color already there
        |
        v
   color targets                      their formats, fixed in the pipeline

Each of the settings on the right comes from the pipeline's blob and is translated by a small function - a vertex format, a topology, a cull mode, a depth comparison:

vertexFormat
/// A vertex attribute's format, as Vulkan's. Vulkan names a format by its components in memory order, each with its
/// size in bits, then how the bits are read: `sfloat` a signed floating-point number, `uint` an unsigned integer,
/// `unorm` an unsigned integer read as a fraction of its largest value (a byte's 0 to 255 as 0.0 to 1.0). So
/// `float32x3`, three 32-bit floats, is `r32g32b32_sfloat`, and `uint8x4_unorm`, four bytes read as fractions - a
/// color - is `r8g8b8a8_unorm`.
fn vertexFormat(format: gpu_abi.VertexFormat) vk.Format {
    return switch (format) {
        .float32 => .r32_sfloat,
        .float32x2 => .r32g32_sfloat,
        .float32x3 => .r32g32b32_sfloat,
        .float32x4 => .r32g32b32a32_sfloat,
        .uint32 => .r32_uint,
        .uint32x2 => .r32g32_uint,
        .uint8x4 => .r8g8b8a8_uint,
        .uint8x4_unorm => .r8g8b8a8_unorm,
    };
}
compareOp
/// A depth comparison, as Vulkan's compare op. The two APIs name the comparisons alike, except that Vulkan spells
/// `less_equal` and `greater_equal` as `less_or_equal` and `greater_or_equal`.
fn compareOp(compare: gpu_abi.CompareFunction) vk.CompareOp {
    return switch (compare) {
        .never => .never,
        .less => .less,
        .equal => .equal,
        .less_equal => .less_or_equal,
        .greater => .greater,
        .not_equal => .not_equal,
        .greater_equal => .greater_or_equal,
        .always => .always,
    };
}

The pipeline itself gathers them all. With dynamic rendering, it also names the formats of the color and depth targets it will draw into, which a pass must then match:

createRenderPipeline
/// A render pipeline from its blob, built as the browser bridge builds its WebGPU pipeline, so the two hosts draw
/// alike. The pipeline is a set of state blocks, each answering one question about the draw:
///
///     shader stages    the vertex and fragment modules, and their entry points
///     vertex input     each vertex buffer's stride and step (its binding is its slot), and each attribute
///     input assembly   how vertices assemble into primitives
///     viewport         dynamic: one viewport and one scissor, set by every pass
///     rasterization    filled polygons, the cull mode, and which winding faces front
///     multisample      the sample count
///     depth            whether depth is tested and written, and the comparison
///     color blend      target 0 blends by the pipeline's mode; further targets are written unblended
///     rendering        the target formats, which every pass using the pipeline must match
fn createRenderPipeline(self: *Host, request: RenderPipelineRequest) BringUpError!u32 {
    const label: []const u8 = request.label;
    const descriptor: gpu_abi.RenderPipelineDescriptor = gpu_abi.decodeRenderPipeline(request.blob) catch |err| {
        panic("vulkan host: render pipeline `{s}`: {s}", .{ label, @errorName(err) });
    };
    // WGSL override constants are a WebGPU feature; the SPIR-V the native host runs has no counterpart for them.
    if (descriptor.constant_count > 0) {
        panic("vulkan host: render pipeline `{s}` sets WGSL override constants, which have no native path", .{label});
    }
    const layout: PipelineLayoutSlot = self.pipeline_layouts.get(request.layout, "pipeline layout").*;

    // Shader stages: each module with its entry point.
    const vertex_module: vk.ShaderModule = self.shader_modules.get(request.vs_module, "shader module").module;
    const fragment_module: vk.ShaderModule = self.shader_modules.get(request.fs_module, "shader module").module;
    const vertex_entry: EntryName = entryName(descriptor.vs_entry_point, label);
    const fragment_entry: EntryName = entryName(descriptor.fs_entry_point, label);
    const stages = [_]vk.PipelineShaderStageCreateInfo{
        .{ .stage = .{ .vertex = true }, .module = vertex_module, .p_name = &vertex_entry },
        .{ .stage = .{ .fragment = true }, .module = fragment_module, .p_name = &fragment_entry },
    };

    // Vertex input: a binding per vertex buffer - its slot, its stride (the bytes from one vertex to the next), and
    // whether it steps per vertex or per instance - and an attribute per shader input, which reads its binding at an
    // offset, in a format, into the input at its location.
    var bindings: [gpu_abi.max_vertex_buffers]vk.VertexInputBindingDescription = undefined;
    var attributes: [gpu_abi.max_vertex_attributes]vk.VertexInputAttributeDescription = undefined;
    var attribute_count: u32 = 0;
    for (descriptor.vertexBuffers(), 0..) |buffer_layout, slot| {
        bindings[slot] = .{
            .binding = @intCast(slot),
            .stride = buffer_layout.array_stride,
            .input_rate = inputRate(buffer_layout.step_mode),
        };
        for (descriptor.attributesOf(buffer_layout)) |attribute| {
            attributes[attribute_count] = .{
                .location = attribute.shader_location,
                .binding = @intCast(slot),
                .format = vertexFormat(attribute.format),
                .offset = attribute.offset,
            };
            attribute_count += 1;
        }
    }
    const vertex_input: vk.PipelineVertexInputStateCreateInfo = .{
        .vertex_binding_description_count = descriptor.vertex_buffer_count,
        .p_vertex_binding_descriptions = &bindings,
        .vertex_attribute_description_count = attribute_count,
        .p_vertex_attribute_descriptions = &attributes,
    };

    // Input assembly: the topology.
    const input_assembly: vk.PipelineInputAssemblyStateCreateInfo = .{
        .topology = primitiveTopology(descriptor.topology),
    };

    // Viewport: dynamic, one viewport and one scissor, which every pass sets when it begins - the viewport with the
    // flip that makes Y point up, as in WebGPU.
    const viewport_state: vk.PipelineViewportStateCreateInfo = .{ .viewport_count = 1, .scissor_count = 1 };

    // Rasterization: filled polygons, the cull mode, and counter-clockwise triangles facing front - WebGPU's default,
    // and still right after the flip, because both APIs judge a triangle's winding in framebuffer coordinates.
    const rasterization: vk.PipelineRasterizationStateCreateInfo = .{
        .polygon_mode = .fill,
        .cull_mode = cullModeFlags(descriptor.cull),
        .front_face = .counter_clockwise,
        .line_width = 1,
    };

    // Multisampling: the sample count, which must equal the targets' (the engine gives both the same count).
    const sample_flags: vk.SampleCountFlags = sampleCountFlags(descriptor.sample_count) orelse
        panic("vulkan host: render pipeline `{s}` asks for {d} samples", .{ label, descriptor.sample_count });
    const multisample: vk.PipelineMultisampleStateCreateInfo = .{ .rasterization_samples = sample_flags };

    // Depth: tested only when the mode tests depth and the pipeline has a depth target to test against - the
    // bridge's rule - and written only when the mode also writes. Without a test, the comparison is `always`.
    const mode_tests_depth: bool = descriptor.depth != .none;
    const has_depth_target: bool = descriptor.depth_format != .undefined_;
    const tests_depth: bool = mode_tests_depth and has_depth_target;
    const writes_depth: bool = tests_depth and descriptor.writes_depth;
    const depth_compare: vk.CompareOp = if (tests_depth) compareOp(descriptor.depth.compareFunction()) else .always;
    const depth_stencil: vk.PipelineDepthStencilStateCreateInfo = .{
        .depth_test_enable = vk.bool32(tests_depth),
        .depth_write_enable = vk.bool32(writes_depth),
        .depth_compare_op = depth_compare,
    };

    // Color blend: target 0 blends by the pipeline's mode; any further targets - several outputs of one fragment
    // shader, written together - are written unblended.
    var blend_attachments: [gpu_abi.max_color_targets]vk.PipelineColorBlendAttachmentState = undefined;
    var color_formats: [gpu_abi.max_color_targets]vk.Format = undefined;
    blend_attachments[0] = blendAttachment(descriptor.blend);
    color_formats[0] = formatInfo(descriptor.color_format).format;
    for (descriptor.extraColorFormats(), 1..) |format, target| {
        blend_attachments[target] = blendAttachment(.none);
        color_formats[target] = formatInfo(format).format;
    }
    const target_count: u32 = 1 + descriptor.extra_color_format_count;
    const color_blend: vk.PipelineColorBlendStateCreateInfo = .{
        .attachment_count = target_count,
        .p_attachments = &blend_attachments,
    };

    // Dynamic state: what each pass sets instead of the pipeline - the viewport and the scissor.
    const dynamic_states = [_]vk.DynamicState{ .viewport, .scissor };
    const dynamic_state: vk.PipelineDynamicStateCreateInfo = .{
        .dynamic_state_count = dynamic_states.len,
        .p_dynamic_states = &dynamic_states,
    };

    // Dynamic rendering: the formats of the targets this pipeline draws into, which every pass using it must match.
    const depth_format: vk.Format = if (tests_depth) formatInfo(descriptor.depth_format).format else .undefined;
    const rendering: vk.PipelineRenderingCreateInfo = .{
        .color_attachment_count = target_count,
        .p_color_attachment_formats = &color_formats,
        .depth_attachment_format = depth_format,
    };
    const pipeline_info: vk.GraphicsPipelineCreateInfo = .{
        .p_next = &rendering,
        .stage_count = stages.len,
        .p_stages = &stages,
        .p_vertex_input_state = &vertex_input,
        .p_input_assembly_state = &input_assembly,
        .p_viewport_state = &viewport_state,
        .p_rasterization_state = &rasterization,
        .p_multisample_state = &multisample,
        .p_depth_stencil_state = &depth_stencil,
        .p_color_blend_state = &color_blend,
        .p_dynamic_state = &dynamic_state,
        .layout = layout.layout,
    };
    var pipeline: vk.Pipeline = .null_handle;
    const pipelines: [*]vk.Pipeline = @ptrCast(&pipeline);
    try vk.check(self.device_dispatch.createGraphicsPipelines(
        self.device,
        .null_handle,
        1,
        @ptrCast(&pipeline_info),
        null,
        pipelines,
    ));
    nameObject(self, .pipeline, @backingInt(pipeline), label);
    retainPipelineLayout(self, request.layout);
    return self.render_pipelines.add(self.gpa, .{
        .pipeline = pipeline,
        .layout = layout.layout,
        .layout_handle = request.layout,
        .set_count = layout.set_count,
        .empty_sets = layout.empty_sets,
    });
}

9.5 Blending

Blending decides the color a pixel ends with when a new color - the source, from the fragment shader - lands on the color already in the target - the destination. Each blend mode is two weighted sums, one for the color channels and one for alpha:

   result.rgb = source.rgb * (source weight) + destination.rgb * (destination weight)
   result.a   = source.a   * (source weight) + destination.a   * (destination weight)

zimr's blend modes are four choices of those weights, the same in both hosts:

/// Color target 0's blending for each zimr blend mode - the factors the browser bridge gives WebGPU, so the two hosts
/// blend alike:
///
///     result.rgb = source.rgb * source_color + destination.rgb * destination_color
///     result.a   = source.a   * source_alpha + destination.a   * destination_alpha
///
/// `alpha` is transparency: the source covers the destination in proportion to its alpha. `additive` adds the
/// source's light, weighted by its alpha. `multiply` scales the destination by the source - shadows, tints.
/// `premultiplied` is transparency for colors already multiplied by their alpha. `none` writes the source as it
/// is. Every mode writes all four channels.
fn blendAttachment(mode: gpu_abi.BlendMode) vk.PipelineColorBlendAttachmentState {
    const all_channels: vk.ColorComponentFlags = .{ .r = true, .g = true, .b = true, .a = true };
    const factors: BlendFactors = switch (mode) {
        .none => return .{ .color_write_mask = all_channels },
        .alpha => .{
            .source_color = .src_alpha,
            .destination_color = .one_minus_src_alpha,
            .source_alpha = .one,
            .destination_alpha = .one_minus_src_alpha,
        },
        .additive => .{
            .source_color = .src_alpha,
            .destination_color = .one,
            .source_alpha = .one,
            .destination_alpha = .one,
        },
        .multiply => .{
            .source_color = .dst_color,
            .destination_color = .zero,
            .source_alpha = .dst_alpha,
            .destination_alpha = .zero,
        },
        .premultiplied => .{
            .source_color = .one,
            .destination_color = .one_minus_src_alpha,
            .source_alpha = .one,
            .destination_alpha = .one_minus_src_alpha,
        },
    };
    return .{
        .blend_enable = vk.bool_true,
        .src_color_blend_factor = factors.source_color,
        .dst_color_blend_factor = factors.destination_color,
        .color_blend_op = .add,
        .src_alpha_blend_factor = factors.source_alpha,
        .dst_alpha_blend_factor = factors.destination_alpha,
        .alpha_blend_op = .add,
        .color_write_mask = all_channels,
    };
}

A worked example with the alpha mode, the usual one for transparency: a half-transparent red, (1, 0, 0) with alpha 0.5, drawn over opaque blue, (0, 0, 1) with alpha 1. The color weights are the source's alpha and one minus it; the alpha weights are one and one minus the source's alpha:

   result.rgb = (1, 0, 0) * 0.5   +  (0, 0, 1) * (1 - 0.5)   =  (0.5, 0, 0.5)    purple
   result.a   = 0.5 * 1           +  1 * (1 - 0.5)           =  1                still opaque

The additive mode adds light instead (glows, sparks); multiply darkens by the source (shadows, tints); premultiplied is the alpha mode for colors whose channels were already multiplied by their alpha.

9.6 The Y axis

A vertex shader places each vertex in clip space, where the visible area runs from -1 to 1 on both axes. WebGPU's clip space has Y pointing up, like a mathematics graph. Vulkan's has Y pointing down, like the rows of an image. A shader written for WebGPU, run unchanged on Vulkan, would draw every frame upside down.

The fix needs no change to any shader. The viewport - the transform from clip space to the target's pixels - may have a negative height in Vulkan. Starting the viewport at the bottom of the target and giving it height -H flips Y on the way to the pixels, and WebGPU's convention comes out exactly. Follow one triangle with corners (0, 0), (1, 0) and (0, 1) in clip space, onto an 800 x 600 target:

   pixel x = (x + 1) / 2 * 800
   pixel y, Vulkan's default:  (y + 1) / 2 * 600      the corner (0, 1) lands at y = 600, the bottom
   pixel y, flipped viewport:  (1 - y) / 2 * 600      the corner (0, 1) lands at y = 0, the top

                     default viewport                  flipped viewport
   (0, 0)  ->        (400, 300)                        (400, 300)
   (1, 0)  ->        (800, 300)                        (800, 300)
   (0, 1)  ->        (400, 600)  pointing down         (400,   0)  pointing up, as on the web

One detail remains: which triangles face the viewer. Both APIs decide it from the winding of a triangle's corners in pixel coordinates, and after the flip the pixel coordinates are exactly WebGPU's - so the pipeline keeps WebGPU's rule, counter-clockwise triangles facing front, unchanged. The flip itself is set when each render pass begins (chapter 10).

10. Render passes and draws

Draws do not happen on their own: each belongs to a render pass, which names the textures being drawn into for its whole length. This chapter follows a pass from its beginning to its end, and then one whole frame of hello_world from the engine's calls down to Vulkan's.

10.1 Targets, load and store

A render pass is a stretch of drawing into a fixed set of targets: a color texture, and usually a depth texture beside it. Fixing the targets for the whole stretch lets the GPU keep them close at hand, and it makes two questions explicit, asked of every target at the pass's two ends. What does it hold when the pass begins - the previous contents (load), or one color everywhere (clear)? And is what was drawn kept when the pass ends (store), or thrown away (discard)?

   begin                          draws                              end
   color:  clear to the            each draw writes the pixels        store: kept for the next pass,
           background color        it covers                          or for the screen
   depth:  clear to 1.0 -          each draw tests against it,        store
           "nothing drawn yet"     and writes it where it wins

Depth is cleared to 1.0, the far end of the depth range, so the first surface drawn at any pixel is nearer than "nothing" and wins the test. A pass may also resolve: when its color target holds several samples per pixel (multisampling, for smoother edges), the pass ends by averaging each pixel's samples into an ordinary texture.

10.2 Beginning a pass

The engine begins a pass with the targets' views and their load and store choices, as WebGPU does:

/// What a render pass is begun with, as the browser's call passes it.
const RenderPassRequest = struct {
    encoder: u32,
    /// The color targets, one to `gpu_abi.max_color_targets`, all the same size.
    color_views: []const u32,
    clear: [4]f32,
    load_op: gpu_abi.LoadOp,
    store_op: gpu_abi.StoreOp,
    /// 0: no depth attachment.
    depth_view: u32,
    /// 0: no multisample resolve. A resolve needs exactly one color target.
    resolve_view: u32,
};

Vulkan's dynamic rendering takes the same information directly: each target's view, its layout, its load and store operations, its clear value. Before beginning, the host records the full barrier of chapter 7, so every earlier write - an upload, a previous pass - is finished and visible. After beginning, it sets the viewport, flipped as chapter 9 showed, and the scissor, which limits drawing to the target's area:

beginRenderPass
/// A render pass, begun as the browser's bridge begins one. Every color target is treated alike: loaded, or cleared
/// to the pass's color; stored, or discarded. With one color target and a resolve target, its samples are averaged
/// into the resolve target at the end. A depth target, when given, is cleared to 1.0 - the far end of the depth
/// range - and stored. A full barrier comes first, as before every pass; the render area, the viewport and the
/// scissor come from the first color target (WebGPU requires every target to be the same size).
fn beginRenderPass(self: *Host, request: RenderPassRequest) BringUpError!u32 {
    const command_buffer: vk.CommandBuffer = self.encoders.get(request.encoder, "command encoder").command_buffer;
    const color_count: usize = request.color_views.len;
    if (color_count == 0 or color_count > gpu_abi.max_color_targets) {
        const limit: usize = gpu_abi.max_color_targets;
        panic("vulkan host: a render pass has {d} color targets (1 to {d})", .{ color_count, limit });
    }
    const has_resolve: bool = request.resolve_view != 0;
    if (has_resolve and color_count != 1) {
        panic("vulkan host: a render pass resolves only with one color target, not {d}", .{color_count});
    }
    const first_color: TextureViewSlot = self.texture_views.get(request.color_views[0], "texture view").*;
    fullBarrier(self, command_buffer);
    var color_attachments: [gpu_abi.max_color_targets]vk.RenderingAttachmentInfo = undefined;
    for (request.color_views, 0..) |view_handle, target| {
        const color: TextureViewSlot = self.texture_views.get(view_handle, "texture view").*;
        color_attachments[target] = .{
            .image_view = color.view,
            .image_layout = .general,
            .load_op = attachmentLoadOp(request.load_op),
            .store_op = attachmentStoreOp(request.store_op),
            .clear_value = .{ .color = .{ .float_32 = request.clear } },
        };
    }
    if (has_resolve) {
        const resolve: TextureViewSlot = self.texture_views.get(request.resolve_view, "texture view").*;
        color_attachments[0].resolve_mode = .{ .average = true };
        color_attachments[0].resolve_image_view = resolve.view;
        color_attachments[0].resolve_image_layout = .general;
    }
    var depth_attachment: vk.RenderingAttachmentInfo = undefined;
    const has_depth: bool = request.depth_view != 0;
    if (has_depth) {
        const depth: TextureViewSlot = self.texture_views.get(request.depth_view, "texture view").*;
        depth_attachment = .{
            .image_view = depth.view,
            .image_layout = .general,
            .load_op = .clear,
            .store_op = .store,
            .clear_value = .{ .depth_stencil = .{ .depth = 1, .stencil = 0 } },
        };
    }
    const rendering_info: vk.RenderingInfo = .{
        .render_area = .{ .extent = first_color.extent },
        .layer_count = 1,
        .color_attachment_count = @intCast(color_count),
        .p_color_attachments = &color_attachments,
        .p_depth_attachment = if (has_depth) &depth_attachment else null,
    };
    self.device_dispatch.cmdBeginRendering(command_buffer, &rendering_info);
    // WebGPU's normalized device coordinates point Y up; Vulkan's point it down. A viewport of negative height,
    // starting at the bottom, flips Y back - and both APIs then agree on where every pixel lands.
    const width: f32 = @floatFromInt(first_color.extent.width);
    const height: f32 = @floatFromInt(first_color.extent.height);
    const viewport: vk.Viewport = .{
        .x = 0,
        .y = height,
        .width = width,
        .height = -height,
        .min_depth = 0,
        .max_depth = 1,
    };
    self.device_dispatch.cmdSetViewport(command_buffer, 0, 1, @ptrCast(&viewport));
    const scissor: vk.Rect2D = .{ .extent = first_color.extent };
    self.device_dispatch.cmdSetScissor(command_buffer, 0, 1, @ptrCast(&scissor));
    return self.render_passes.add(self.gpa, .{ .command_buffer = command_buffer });
}

A pass may draw into several color targets at once. The fragment shader then writes one color per target - its outputs are numbered by location, @location(0) going to the first target and @location(1) to the second - and every draw fills all of them. Deferred rendering is built on this: a first pass writes each pixel's surface color, direction and position into three targets, together called a G-buffer, and a second pass lights every pixel from what they hold.

   fragment output    the pass's target          holds, for each pixel
   ---------------    -----------------------    ---------------------------------
   @location(0)       color_views[0], albedo     the surface's own color
   @location(1)       color_views[1], normal     the direction the surface faces
   @location(2)       color_views[2], position   where the surface is
                      the depth target           the depth test, as with one target

The host takes the targets as a list - a pass with one target is a list of one - and treats them alike, as the browser's bridge does: one clear color, one load choice and one store choice for all of them. WebGPU requires every target of a pass to have the same size, so the render area, the viewport and the scissor come from the first. A resolve (section 10.1) is offered with a single target only.

10.3 Draw commands

Between beginning and end, the engine sets state and draws. Setting a pipeline and buffers is immediate: each becomes one Vulkan command. Bind groups need one more step, because of a rule the two APIs do not share. WebGPU lets a program set a bind group before the pipeline that will use it; Vulkan binds a set against a pipeline layout, so a set bound before a pipeline change may have to be bound again. The host therefore only records each bind group as it is set, and binds every group set since the last draw at the next draw, against the pipeline bound then. A pass remembers which group slots need binding:

/// One bit per bind-group slot of a pass.
const GroupSet = std.bit_set.IntegerBitSet(max_bind_groups);
RenderPassSlot
/// A render or compute pass being recorded into its encoder's command buffer. Bind groups are recorded as they are
/// set and bound at the next draw or dispatch, against the layout of the pipeline bound then - WebGPU lets a group be
/// set before the pipeline it will be used with.
const PassSlot = struct {
    command_buffer: vk.CommandBuffer,
    /// Which pipelines and groups the pass binds: a render pass's, or a compute pass's.
    bind_point: vk.PipelineBindPoint = .graphics,
    pipeline_layout: vk.PipelineLayout = .null_handle,
    pipeline_set_count: u32 = 0,
    /// The bound pipeline's sets with no bindings, which no group is bound to.
    pipeline_empty_sets: GroupSet = .empty,
    groups: [max_bind_groups]vk.DescriptorSet = @splat(.null_handle),
    /// The group slots to bind at the next draw: set since the last draw bound them, or since a pipeline change.
    dirty_groups: GroupSet = .empty,
    /// A compute pass: whether a dispatch is recorded already. Each later one waits for the writes before it.
    has_dispatched: bool = false,
};

Setting a pipeline marks every slot as needing a bind; setting a group records it and marks its slot:

/// Binds a render pipeline for the pass's next draws.
pub fn renderPassSetPipeline(
    pass: u32,
    pipeline: u32,
) void {
    const self: *Host = theHost();
    const slot: *PassSlot = self.render_passes.get(pass, "render pass");
    const bound: PipelineSlot = self.render_pipelines.get(pipeline, "render pipeline").*;
    self.device_dispatch.cmdBindPipeline(slot.command_buffer, .graphics, bound.pipeline);
    // Every group binds again at the next draw, against this pipeline's layout.
    slot.pipeline_layout = bound.layout;
    slot.pipeline_set_count = bound.set_count;
    slot.pipeline_empty_sets = bound.empty_sets;
    slot.dirty_groups = .full;
}
render_pass_set_bind_group
/// Sets a bind group for the pass's next draws. It is bound at the next draw, against the pipeline bound then.
pub fn renderPassSetBindGroup(
    pass: u32,
    group_index: u32,
    bind_group: u32,
) void {
    // A group of handle 0 is skipped, as the bridge skips it.
    if (bind_group == 0) {
        return;
    }
    const self: *Host = theHost();
    if (group_index >= max_bind_groups) {
        panic("vulkan host: bind group index {d}; zimr binds at most {d}", .{ group_index, max_bind_groups });
    }
    const slot: *PassSlot = self.render_passes.get(pass, "render pass");
    slot.groups[group_index] = self.bind_groups.get(bind_group, "bind group").descriptor_set.set;
    slot.dirty_groups.set(group_index);
}

and a draw first binds what is marked, then draws:

/// Binds every group set since the last draw or dispatch (or since the pipeline changed) against the bound pipeline's
/// layout, at the pass's bind point.
///
/// A set whose layout in the pipeline is empty gets nothing. WebGPU lets such a slot keep whatever group was bound
/// there before - the engine's shader runtime fills the slots below a shader's own groups with empty layouts, and the
/// 2D renderer's groups stay bound in them - since no shader can read an empty set. Vulkan requires a bound set to
/// match the pipeline's set layout, and needs nothing bound where the shaders read nothing; so the host binds nothing
/// there.
fn bindDirtyGroups(self: *Host, pass: *PassSlot) void {
    const no_dynamic_offsets = [_]u32{};
    for (0..pass.pipeline_set_count) |index| {
        const set: vk.DescriptorSet = pass.groups[index];
        const is_empty_in_pipeline: bool = pass.pipeline_empty_sets.isSet(index);
        if (!pass.dirty_groups.isSet(index) or set == .null_handle or is_empty_in_pipeline) {
            continue;
        }
        const sets = [_]vk.DescriptorSet{set};
        self.device_dispatch.cmdBindDescriptorSets(
            pass.command_buffer,
            pass.bind_point,
            pass.pipeline_layout,
            @intCast(index),
            1,
            &sets,
            0,
            &no_dynamic_offsets,
        );
        pass.dirty_groups.unset(index);
    }
}

One kind of slot gets nothing. The engine's shader runtime lays out a custom shader's groups from 0 to the highest it uses, and fills the slots below its own with empty layouts; when such a shader draws, the 2D renderer's groups are still bound in those slots. WebGPU accepts that, since no shader can read an empty slot. Vulkan requires a bound set to match the pipeline's layout at its slot, and needs nothing bound where the shaders read nothing - so where the bound pipeline's layout is empty, the host binds nothing.

render_pass_draw_indexed
/// Draws indexed primitives, first binding the groups set since the last draw.
pub fn renderPassDrawIndexed(
    pass: u32,
    index_count: u32,
    instance_count: u32,
    first_index: u32,
    base_vertex: i32,
    first_instance: u32,
) void {
    const self: *Host = theHost();
    const slot: *PassSlot = self.render_passes.get(pass, "render pass");
    bindDirtyGroups(self, slot);
    self.device_dispatch.cmdDrawIndexed(
        slot.command_buffer,
        index_count,
        instance_count,
        first_index,
        base_vertex,
        first_instance,
    );
}

A draw takes its vertices in one of two ways. An indexed draw reads an index buffer and fetches the vertices it names, so a vertex shared by several triangles is stored once. A draw without indices takes the vertices in order, three to a triangle, and needs no index buffer. A pass that covers the whole target can draw a single triangle large enough to cover it this way, with no vertex buffer either: its vertex shader computes the three corners from the vertex's index. Both kinds of draw bind the marked groups first:

/// Draws primitives without an index buffer - vertices taken in order - first binding the groups set since the last
/// draw.
pub fn renderPassDraw(
    pass: u32,
    vertex_count: u32,
    instance_count: u32,
    first_vertex: u32,
    first_instance: u32,
) void {
    const self: *Host = theHost();
    const slot: *PassSlot = self.render_passes.get(pass, "render pass");
    bindDirtyGroups(self, slot);
    self.device_dispatch.cmdDraw(slot.command_buffer, vertex_count, instance_count, first_vertex, first_instance);
}

10.4 The scissor rectangle

The scissor rectangle limits a pass's draws to part of the target: no draw changes a pixel outside it. (The clear at the pass's beginning is not a draw, and covers the whole target whatever the scissor.) A user interface uses it to clip a scrolling panel's contents to the panel; a split-screen game, to keep each player's view in its own half. A pass begins with the whole target as its scissor (section 10.2), and the engine may set another between draws.

The rectangle is in the target's pixels, counted from its top-left corner, in WebGPU and in Vulkan alike, so it passes through the host unchanged. The Y flip of section 9.6 does not affect it. The flip belongs to the viewport, which decides where a shape's coordinates land on the target; the scissor test comes after, on the target's own pixels.

   a target of 800 x 600 pixels, and the scissor x 100, y 50, width 200, height 100

         x=0                                                     x=800
   y=0    +-------------------------------------------------------+
          |                                                       |
   y=50   |       +--------------+                                |
          |       | drawn        |                                |
   y=150  |       +--------------+                                |
          |     x=100          x=300                              |
          |                                                       |
   y=600  +-------------------------------------------------------+

   Both APIs keep the pixels from x 100 to 299 and from y 50 to 149, counted from the top.
   The viewport places each shape - flipped, so that WebGPU's up stays up - and the
   scissor then keeps only the pixels inside the rectangle.
/// Clips the pass's draws to a rectangle of the target, in pixels from its top-left corner. Both APIs place that
/// corner the same way - zimr's Y flip is the viewport's alone - so the rectangle passes through unchanged.
pub fn renderPassSetScissorRect(
    pass: u32,
    x: u32,
    y: u32,
    w: u32,
    h: u32,
) void {
    const self: *Host = theHost();
    const slot: *PassSlot = self.render_passes.get(pass, "render pass");
    const scissor: vk.Rect2D = .{
        .offset = .{ .x = @intCast(x), .y = @intCast(y) },
        .extent = .{ .width = w, .height = h },
    };
    self.device_dispatch.cmdSetScissor(slot.command_buffer, 0, 1, @ptrCast(&scissor));
}

10.5 Ending a pass

Ending a pass is one command. Nothing else is needed at this point: the store and resolve choices were made when the pass began, and the next pass's full barrier orders whatever follows:

/// Ends the render pass.
pub fn renderPassEnd(
    pass: u32,
) void {
    const self: *Host = theHost();
    const slot: PassSlot = self.render_passes.remove(self.gpa, pass, "render pass") catch outOfMemory();
    self.device_dispatch.cmdEndRendering(slot.command_buffer);
}

10.6 A complete frame

The table below follows a frame of hello_world - the background cleared, a few shapes and some text drawn - from the engine's calls, through the Vulkan host, to the Vulkan commands they become. It shows one batch of draws, where the engine may draw several:

   engine (through wgpu.zig)        Vulkan host                     Vulkan
   -----------------------------    ----------------------------    ------------------------------------------
   getCurrentTextureView            surfaceGetCurrentTexture        wait for this slot's last present;
                                                                    vkAcquireNextImageKHR; into uploads:
                                                                    the image to GENERAL, cleared to zeros
   createCommandEncoder             deviceCreateCommandEncoder      vkBeginCommandBuffer
   render_pass.begin(background)    encoderBeginRenderPass          full barrier; vkCmdBeginRendering;
                                                                    vkCmdSetViewport (flipped); vkCmdSetScissor
   queueWriteBuffer(projection)     queueWriteBuffer                staged; into uploads: barrier, copy
   setPipeline(shapes)              renderPassSetPipeline           vkCmdBindPipeline; every group marked
   setBindGroup(0, projection)      renderPassSetBindGroup          recorded, marked
   setBindGroup(1, texture)         renderPassSetBindGroup          recorded, marked
   queueWriteBuffer(vertices)       queueWriteBuffer                staged; into uploads: barrier, copy
   setVertexBuffer, setIndexBuffer  renderPassSet...Buffer          vkCmdBindVertexBuffers, vkCmdBindIndexBuffer
   drawIndexed(n)                   renderPassDrawIndexed           vkCmdBindDescriptorSets (groups 0 and 1);
                                                                    vkCmdDrawIndexed
   render_pass.end                  renderPassEnd                   vkCmdEndRendering
   finishCommandEncoder, submit     commandEncoderFinish,           vkEndCommandBuffer; uploads ended;
                                    queueSubmit                     vkQueueSubmit2: [uploads | encoder],
                                                                    waiting for the acquire, signalling #n
   surfacePresent                   surfacePresent                  a command buffer: the image to PRESENT_SRC;
                                                                    vkQueueSubmit2 signalling its present
                                                                    semaphore; vkQueuePresentKHR

The writes made during the frame - the projection, the vertices - go into uploads rather than the frame's command buffer, and uploads runs first, so each write lands before the draws that read it. The engine's column mentions no image layout, semaphore or barrier: what WebGPU keeps implicit stays implicit for the engine, and the host supplies it.

11. Windows

On Windows, the browser's part is played by two small files: win32.zig, which owns the window and speaks to the operating system, and native_loop.zig, which runs the frames. This chapter follows what they do in the order a program meets it: the window, the loop that draws into it, the two kinds of pixel, and the input the user gives it.

11.1 The window

A Windows program learns what happens to its window through messages: small records - a number naming the event, and two parameters - that Windows queues for the window and hands over one at a time. A key going down is a message, a mouse move is one, a click on the close button is one. The program supplies a function, the window procedure, and Windows calls it once for each message.

Zig's standard library declares no windowing functions, so win32.zig declares the few that zimr needs by hand, with Windows' own names and types, and the build checks every declaration against the system's headers. Its Window opens one window and records, from the messages, the facts the frame loop must react to:

   message           the window records          the frame loop
   ---------------   -------------------------   ------------------------------------------
   WM_CLOSE          close_requested             stops
   WM_SIZE, zero     minimized                   draws nothing until the window returns
   WM_SIZE           size_changed                rebuilds the swapchain before its next frame
   WM_DPICHANGED     the new scale               (the size follows it: see 11.3)

Messages are taken off the queue once per frame by pump. Each is first translated - a key message may produce a second message carrying the character it types (section 11.6) - and then dispatched to the window procedure:

/// Handles every waiting message, then returns: call once per frame.
pub fn pump(window: *Window) void {
    _ = window;
    var message: MSG = undefined;
    while (PeekMessageW(&message, null, 0, 0, PM_REMOVE) != 0) {
        _ = TranslateMessage(&message);
        _ = DispatchMessageW(&message);
    }
}

11.2 The frame loop

In the browser, the page asks for frames with requestAnimationFrame, and the browser calls the program back once per display refresh. On Windows, native_loop.run plays that part: each pass takes every waiting message, hands the input they carried to the engine, and calls the program's update - the same function the browser calls.

   browser                                      native_loop.run
   ------------------------------------------   ------------------------------------------
   events arrive between frames                 pump: every waiting message, in order
   requestAnimationFrame calls update           the input delivered, then update called
   a hidden tab gets no frames                  a minimized window gets no frames
   a frame waits for the display                FIFO presentation waits for the display

Nothing in the loop sets its pace; presentation does. With FIFO presentation (section 7.5), presenting a frame waits until the display has taken the previous one, so the loop runs at the display's refresh rate, as requestAnimationFrame does. A minimized window has no visible pixels, and the loop sleeps instead of drawing.

/// Runs the window's frames until it is closed, each at the time it starts on the system's clock - or, when the
/// `ZIMR_SCRIPT` setting names a script, runs the script in the window (`runScriptInWindow`) and returns how it went.
pub fn run(
    update: UpdateFunction,
    receive_event: EventFunction,
    capture: script.CaptureFunction,
) ?script.Outcome {
    if (comptime !has_window_loop) {
        // No window was opened (`openWindow`): no frames to run.
        return null;
    }
    reported_size = size();
    var setting_buffer: [512]u8 = undefined;
    if (native_os.environmentVariable("ZIMR_SCRIPT", &setting_buffer)) |script_path| {
        return runScriptInWindow(update, receive_event, capture, script_path);
    }
    while (true) {
        const pass: WindowPass = switch (serviceWindow(receive_event)) {
            // The frame's wait, here, input handed over as it arrives (`waitForFrame`).
            .draw => waitForFrame(receive_event),
            else => |other| other,
        };
        switch (pass) {
            .draw => update(native_os.monotonicMilliseconds()),
            .hidden => win32.Sleep(16),
            .closed => return null,
        }
    }
}

11.3 Logical and physical pixels

Screens differ in how many pixels they pack into an inch, so Windows lets the user scale everything up: at 150%, a button meant to be 100 pixels wide is drawn 150 pixels wide. zimr works in logical pixels - the browser's CSS pixels, the same size on every screen - and the window converts at its edges. The ratio between the two is the window's scale: its DPI (dots per inch) over the standard 96.

/// Physical pixels per logical pixel - the window's DPI over the standard 96: 1.0 at 100% scale, 1.5 at 150%.
pub fn scale(window: *const Window) f32 {
    const dpi: f32 = @floatFromInt(window.dpi);
    const default_dpi: f32 = @floatFromInt(USER_DEFAULT_SCREEN_DPI);
    return dpi / default_dpi;
}

At 150% the DPI is 144, and the scale 1.5. Following a window through it:

   the program asks for                 1280 x 720 logical pixels
   the window's client area             1920 x 1080 physical pixels      (times 1.5)
   the swapchain's images               1920 x 1080                      every physical pixel drawn
   a click Windows reports at           (300, 150) physical
   the engine receives                  (200, 100) logical               (divided by 1.5)

The process declares itself per-monitor DPI aware when the window opens. Without that declaration, Windows would draw the window at 100% and stretch the result, blurring it. Being aware means the program scales for itself and is told when the scale changes: a window dragged onto a monitor with another scale receives WM_DPICHANGED, with a new outer size that Windows suggests at the new scale, and takes it.

11.4 The input path

The engine receives input as events - a key down or up, typed text, the mouse's position, a button, the wheel - in one function, and does not know where they come from (section 13.2). In the browser, the functions the bridge calls on DOM events build them. On Windows, every input message travels a short path to the same function:

   Windows               win32.zig                native_input.zig          native_loop.zig
   -------------------   ----------------------   -----------------------   ---------------------------
   a message:            the window procedure     translate: the message    before each frame, each
   WM_KEYDOWN, WM_CHAR,  does Windows' own        becomes input events,     event in order, as the
   WM_MOUSEMOVE, ...     part, then hands it on   queued                    contract's event, to the
                         with the window's scale                            engine (receiveEvent)

The split gives each file one job. win32.zig knows Windows and nothing of the engine. native_input.zig knows the engine's events and, of Windows, only the numbers of its messages - so every rule in it can be tested on any computer, from a message's own bits. native_loop.zig delivers. The translation is one function:

/// Turns one of the window's input messages into the engine's events, queued in order. The window procedure has
/// done Windows' own part of the message already (`win32.Window.forwardInput`).
pub fn translate(message: win32.InputMessage, queue: *InputQueue) void {
    const parameter_bits: usize = @bitCast(message.l_param);
    switch (message.message) {
        win32.WM_KEYDOWN, win32.WM_SYSKEYDOWN => translateKeyDown(parameter_bits, message.label, queue),
        win32.WM_KEYUP, win32.WM_SYSKEYUP => translateKeyUp(message.w_param, parameter_bits, queue),
        win32.WM_CHAR => queue.pushUtf16(@truncate(message.w_param)),
        win32.WM_MOUSEMOVE => {
            const kind: contract.PointerKind = pointerKindOfExtraInfo(message.extra_info);
            queue.push(.{ .mouse_move = positionOf(parameter_bits, message.scale, kind) });
        },
        win32.WM_LBUTTONDOWN,
        win32.WM_RBUTTONDOWN,
        win32.WM_MBUTTONDOWN,
        win32.WM_XBUTTONDOWN,
        win32.WM_LBUTTONUP,
        win32.WM_RBUTTONUP,
        win32.WM_MBUTTONUP,
        win32.WM_XBUTTONUP,
        => translateButton(message, queue),
        win32.WM_MOUSEWHEEL => {
            const notches: f32 = wheelNotches(win32.signedHighWord(message.w_param));
            queue.push(.{ .mouse_wheel = .{ .horizontal = 0, .vertical = notches } });
        },
        win32.WM_MOUSEHWHEEL => {
            const notches: f32 = wheelNotches(win32.signedHighWord(message.w_param));
            queue.push(.{ .mouse_wheel = .{ .horizontal = notches, .vertical = 0 } });
        },
        win32.WM_SETFOCUS => queue.push(.focus_gained),
        win32.WM_KILLFOCUS => queue.push(.focus_lost),
        win32.message_pointer_entered => queue.push(.mouse_enter),
        win32.WM_MOUSELEAVE => queue.push(.mouse_leave),
        win32.message_pointer_motion => {
            const horizontal: isize = @bitCast(message.w_param);
            const vertical: isize = message.l_param;
            const horizontal_pixels: f32 = @floatFromInt(horizontal);
            const vertical_pixels: f32 = @floatFromInt(vertical);
            queue.push(.{ .pointer_motion = .{
                .dx = horizontal_pixels / message.scale,
                .dy = vertical_pixels / message.scale,
            } });
        },
        win32.message_pointer_lock_changed => queue.push(.{ .pointer_lock_changed = message.w_param != 0 }),
        win32.message_fullscreen_changed => queue.push(.{ .fullscreen_changed = message.w_param != 0 }),
        win32.message_paste => queue.push(.{ .paste = message.text }),
        else => {},
    }
}

Each rule in it matches what the bridge sends, so the engine cannot tell the two platforms apart. The next sections take the rules one kind of input at a time.

11.5 Keys

A key event names a physical key - a position on the keyboard - not the letter printed on it. In the browser this is KeyboardEvent.code: the key to the right of Caps Lock is "KeyA" on every keyboard, though a French keyboard prints Q on it. Games want positions: four keys laid out under the left hand stay under the left hand, whatever the keyboard's language.
