A request asks the platform for something outside the window's contents. Some take effect at once: a title, a cursor, a screen kept on, a page opened. Others are granted in the platform's time, and the outcome comes back as an event: a browser grants fullscreen or a pointer lock only during a click or a key press, and ends either when the user presses Escape. The engine keeps the last outcome reported - f.window.fullscreen, isPointerLocked - so an app asks, then reads what holds.

   request             the browser                       Windows
   ------------------  --------------------------------  ----------------------------------------------
   setTitle            document.title                    SetWindowTextW
   setCursor           the canvas's CSS cursor           a system cursor, kept by WM_SETCURSOR
   requestFullscreen   the Fullscreen API, then          borderless: the frame removed, the window
                       fullscreenchange                  stretched over its monitor; placement restored
   requestPointerLock  the Pointer Lock API, then        raw input, the cursor held at the centre
                       pointerlockchange                 and hidden
   keepAwake           the Screen Wake Lock API, asked   SetThreadExecutionState
                       again whenever the page returns
   openUrl             a new tab                         ShellExecuteW - http, https and mailto only
   setClipboardText    the Clipboard API                 CF_UNICODETEXT, owned by the window
   requestPaste        clipboard.readText, then paste    CF_UNICODETEXT read, then paste
   (Ctrl+V)            the page's paste event            Ctrl+V or Shift+Insert, read as the paste command
   saveValue           localStorage, base64-encoded      a file per key under %APPDATA%\zimr\<program>\,
                                                         written beside it and moved over it

A paste reaches the engine as an event either way: from the system's own paste command, Ctrl+V, or as the outcome of requestPaste, for a paste button. A browser carries the text in the page's own paste event, readable without the Clipboard API's permission; Windows has no such event for a window that is not a text box, so the window reads Ctrl+V and Shift+Insert itself and pastes as a browser would. The engine keeps the text for one frame (getPastedText).

Saved values are bytes, kept as they went in. The browser's storage holds text, so a value is stored there base64-encoded; Windows keeps each value as a file, written in full beside the old one and then moved over it in one step, so a crash in the middle of a save leaves the previous value whole.

The cursor is a wish rather than a call. setMouseCursor, hideCursor and disableCursor record in the input state what the app wants; after the frame's update, the engine shows the last wish through the platform, and only when it differs from what is shown. Any code may ask - the UI asks on every hover - and a frame that asks many times costs one call:

/// Shows the cursor the app asked for during the frame - its shape (`setMouseCursor`), or none while it is hidden
/// (`hideCursor`, `disableCursor`) - through the platform, when it differs from the one shown. Any code may ask, the
/// UI included, and the last wish of the frame is the one shown.
fn showWantedCursor(app: *App) void {
    const is_hidden: bool = input.isCursorHidden(&app.input_state);
    const shape: contract.Cursor = contractCursorOf(input.getMouseCursor(&app.input_state));
    const wanted: contract.Cursor = if (is_hidden) .hidden else shape;
    if (wanted == app.shown_cursor) {
        return;
    }
    app.shown_cursor = wanted;
    platform.current.setCursor(wanted);
}

In a browser, fullscreen depends on the device. Desktop browsers, Android's and Safari on iPad offer the Fullscreen API - under its standard names, or on iPad before iPadOS 16.4 with the webkit prefix; the engine tries the standard name first and the prefixed one after, and listens for both fullscreenchange and webkitfullscreenchange. Safari on iPhone has none: only a video can go fullscreen there. What an iPhone offers instead is the web app: a page added to the Home Screen and opened from its icon runs without the browser's bars. The page's head asks for that mode (apple-mobile-web-app-capable; since iOS 26 every page added to the Home Screen opens so), lets the page reach under a translucent status bar (apple-mobile-web-app-status-bar-style) and around a notch (viewport-fit=cover), and the canvas fills the screen's safe area - the part that no notch, status bar or home indicator covers, whose insets CSS gives as env(safe-area-inset-left) and its three siblings:

   an iPhone in landscape, the page opened from the Home Screen

   +------+----------------------------------------------+------+
   |      |                                              |      |
   |  n   |                                              |      |
   |  o   |                  the canvas                  |      |
   |  t   |        the safe area: the screen less        |      |
   |  c   |      a strip on each side, for the notch,    |      |
   |  h   |       and one at the bottom, for the bar     |      |
   |      |                                              |      |
   |      +----------------------------------------------+      |
   |      |               home indicator                 |      |
   +------+----------------------------------------------+------+
     left inset                                      right inset

Pointer positions are measured from the canvas's own rectangle, so a canvas moved in by the insets still receives them in its own pixels. The page's fullscreen button follows the same lines: it toggles the Fullscreen API where there is one; where there is none, a tap explains how to add the page to the Home Screen; opened from the Home Screen, the button hides. A request the browser refuses - outside a gesture, or in a page shown inside another that does not allow fullscreen - is shown with its reason and written to the page's log.

Fullscreen on Windows is borderless rather than a change of display mode: the window's frame is removed and the window covers its monitor, so switching is immediate and the desktop around it stays as it was. The window's placement is saved first - its position, size, and whether it was maximized - and given back when fullscreen ends:

/// Fullscreen as borderless windowed: the window's frame removed and the window stretched over its monitor; the
/// frame and the place it had are given back when fullscreen ends. Switching is immediate and needs no change of
/// display mode, so other windows and the desktop stay as they were. The outcome is reported as input
/// (`message_fullscreen_changed`), as a browser reports `fullscreenchange`.
pub fn setFullscreen(window: *Window, on: bool) void {
    const handle: HWND = window.handle orelse return;
    if (on == window.is_fullscreen) {
        window.forwardInput(message_fullscreen_changed, @intFromBool(on), 0);
        return;
    }
    const style: LONG_PTR = GetWindowLongPtrW(handle, GWL_STYLE);
    const frame: LONG_PTR = WS_OVERLAPPEDWINDOW;
    if (on) {
        var monitor_info: MONITORINFO = zeroes(MONITORINFO);
        monitor_info.cbSize = @sizeOf(MONITORINFO);
        window.placement_before_fullscreen.length = @sizeOf(WINDOWPLACEMENT);
        const monitor: *anyopaque = MonitorFromWindow(handle, MONITOR_DEFAULTTONEAREST) orelse return;
        const has_placement: bool = GetWindowPlacement(handle, &window.placement_before_fullscreen) != 0;
        if (!has_placement or GetMonitorInfoW(monitor, &monitor_info) == 0) {
            window.forwardInput(message_fullscreen_changed, 0, 0);
            return;
        }
        const area: RECT = monitor_info.rcMonitor;
        _ = SetWindowLongPtrW(handle, GWL_STYLE, style & ~frame);
        const flags: UINT = SWP_NOOWNERZORDER | SWP_FRAMECHANGED;
        _ = SetWindowPos(handle, null, area.left, area.top, area.right - area.left, area.bottom - area.top, flags);
    } else {
        _ = SetWindowLongPtrW(handle, GWL_STYLE, style | frame);
        _ = SetWindowPlacement(handle, &window.placement_before_fullscreen);
        const flags: UINT = SWP_NOMOVE | SWP_NOSIZE | SWP_NOZORDER | SWP_NOOWNERZORDER | SWP_FRAMECHANGED;
        _ = SetWindowPos(handle, null, 0, 0, 0, 0, flags);
    }
    window.is_fullscreen = on;
    window.forwardInput(message_fullscreen_changed, @intFromBool(on), 0);
}

Opening an address is narrower on Windows than the shell's own open, which would also start programs and open files: only http, https and mailto addresses are passed on, as a browser's window.open only ever opens pages. A platform without a window - the headless one - declines every request: nothing is shown, and no outcome is reported.

The engine's raylib-style calls go the same way. setWindowTitle, openURL and setClipboardText are requests of the contract, carried out by the platform the program was built for, so they work in a window and headless as in the browser; and every clock of the runtime reads one clock, the platform's now() - performance.now() in the browser, the performance counter on Windows, the monotonic clock elsewhere. raylib is built the same way: each of its platforms is a file, after its template rcore_template.c, defining SetWindowTitle, SetClipboardText, GetClipboardText, OpenURL and GetTime for the core to call. A question an engine asks about where it runs is a question about the host - is this the browser? - and is answered by the host's facts (host.is_browser), not by the processor it was compiled for.

13.9 Text fields

A text field is edited in two ways. In the browser, the field the UI draws is covered, while it is active, by an element of the page - an <input>, or a <textarea> for several lines - so the browser's own editor does the editing: selection, undo, input methods, a phone's keyboard. The UI reads the text back each frame. Natively, zimr edits the text itself, with an editing core that knows nothing of drawing or keys: the UI turns keys and clicks into its moves and edits.

In the browser, the field reads the page's text back each frame and compares it with its own, whole: any difference is an edit, even one that keeps the text's length, a character replaced by another. The edit is found by what the two texts share - the bytes both begin with and the bytes both end with stay as they are, and the new bytes between them are what the browser's editor inserted, whether typed, pasted or committed by an input method. Those characters go through the field's filters, its char_filter callback among them, as a native field's typed characters do; when the filters change anything, the page's field gets the filtered text back, its caret after the insertion:

   field's text      a b                  shared start  a
   browser's text    a 1 x b              shared end    b
   inserted            1 x                filtered      1   (digits only)
   field's text      a 1 b                caret after the 1

Every position in the core is a byte offset into the UTF-8 text, always at a character boundary. A character is one to four bytes, and every byte after a character's first is a continuation byte, 10xxxxxx; stepping over continuation bytes finds the next boundary, so the caret never stands inside a character:

   text      a    é         €            😀
   bytes     61   C3 A9      E2 82 AC      F0 9F 98 80
   offsets   0    1          3             6             10

A selection is the text between two offsets: the anchor, where it began, and the caret, where it ends now. A move with Shift held moves only the caret, so the selection grows or shrinks around the anchor; a move without Shift brings the anchor along, and nothing is selected. Typing, pasting and deleting replace the selection first.

A word move stops where a word begins: a word character after a blank or a punctuation mark, or the start of a run of punctuation - the rule Dear ImGui uses, with the punctuation of Chinese and Japanese text among the marks. In a password field the text has no words, so a word move cannot reveal where its hidden spaces are:

   h e l l o ,   w o r l d     f o o
   ^         ^   ^             ^     ^      the stops of word moves
   0         5   7             14    17
/// Whether a word begins at `offset`: a word character after a blank or a separator, or the first of a run of
/// separators. Never at the text's start, which word moves treat as their own stop.
fn isWordStart(text_bytes: []const u8, offset: usize) bool {
    if (offset == 0 or offset >= text_bytes.len) {
        return false;
    }
    const previous: u21 = characterAt(text_bytes, previousBoundary(text_bytes, offset));
    const current: u21 = characterAt(text_bytes, offset);
    const previous_ends_word: bool = isBlank(previous) or isSeparator(previous);
    const current_is_word: bool = !isBlank(current) and !isSeparator(current);
    const separators_begin: bool = isSeparator(current) and !isSeparator(previous);
    return (previous_ends_word and current_is_word) or separators_begin;
}

Every edit is recorded as a step - where it happened, the bytes it removed and the bytes it inserted, and where the caret and anchor were - so undo puts the removed bytes back in place of the inserted ones, and redo does the opposite. Typing is recorded a word at a time: a typed character joins the step being typed, unless it is a blank after a word, which begins a new step. So undo takes back world, then the space, then hello, as a text editor does. When the field became active its text was kept, and Escape restores it, as one more undoable step.

The keys a native field answers are Dear ImGui's. A paste needs no key handling of its own: Ctrl+V and Shift+Insert reach the field as the platform's paste (section 13.8).

   Left, Right               a character            Ctrl+Left, Ctrl+Right      a word
   Home, End                 the text's ends        Shift with any move        extends the selection
   Backspace, Delete         a character            Ctrl+Backspace, Ctrl+Del   a word
   Ctrl+A                    select all             Ctrl+Z                     undo
   Ctrl+C, Ctrl+Insert       copy                   Ctrl+Y, Ctrl+Shift+Z       redo
   Ctrl+X, Shift+Delete      cut                    Escape                     revert, or clear, and leave
   Shift+click               extend the selection

   in a field of several lines:
   Up, Down                  a line                 Home, End                  the line's ends
   Page Up, Page Down        the lines shown        Ctrl+Home, Ctrl+End        the text's ends
   Enter                     a line break

Up and Down keep the caret's x rather than its count of characters: the x is measured when a run of vertical moves begins and kept while it lasts, so a caret passing through a short line comes back to its column on the next long one, wherever the characters' widths put it.

The mouse selects as in any text editor. A press places the caret and, while the button stays held, the caret follows the mouse with the anchor left where the press landed. Clicks in quick succession on one spot are counted - within 0.3 seconds and 6 pixels of the last, Dear ImGui's thresholds - so a second click selects the word under the mouse and a third the whole line.

Text longer than its field scrolls so the caret stays in view. When the caret leaves the view, the text moves a quarter of the field's width further than needed, so typing at the end scrolls once every few characters rather than at each; and the view never scrolls past the text's end, so deleting brings the text back into view:

   |the quick brown fo|x jumps            the caret leaves the view on the right
   |  quick brown fox jum|ps              the text moves a quarter of the field further
   ^ the field's left edge

A field of several lines scrolls both ways: vertically just as far as brings the caret's line inside, and horizontally by the quarter steps of a field of one line. Its scroll is kept per field, in the UI's per-widget storage, so a field keeps its place when another is active, and the wheel scrolls whichever field is under the mouse - five font heights a notch, as a child window - taking the wheel's turn from the window around it. The scroll follows the caret only when the caret moves, so a place reached with the wheel stays until the next edit or move.

A password field copies and cuts nothing. With Ctrl held and Alt not, a key is a shortcut and types nothing; Ctrl and Alt together are AltGr, which types the characters of many keyboard layouts.

Not every typed or pasted character belongs in a field. Control characters stay out, except a line break in a field of several lines and a tab where tabs are allowed; so does DEL, which some systems send for Backspace, and, when typed, the private-use range of Unicode, where some systems put special keys. A line break pasted into a field of one line becomes a space, so the pasted words stay apart.

13.10 Input methods

Chinese, Japanese and Korean are typed through an input method: keys build a composition - a reading, a few syllables - which the method turns into candidates, and the chosen text is committed to the field. The method draws its own windows, the composition being typed and the list of candidates, and needs to know where the caret is to put them beside it.

A text field that is active and edited natively asks for text input each frame, with its kind and its area: the field's box and the caret's distance from its left edge (for a field of several lines, the caret's line). The request lasts the frame, and the engine compares it with the last one it gave the platform:

   frame's request       last given           the platform is told
   ---------------       ----------           --------------------
   field A at x          none                 startTextInput(A's kind), setTextInputArea(x)
   field A at x'         field A at x         setTextInputArea(x')
   none                  field A              stopTextInput()

On Windows the input method is off from the window's opening: its keys would otherwise start compositions in the middle of a game. startTextInput associates the window with its default input method context, except for a password, whose keys go straight to the field as a system's own password boxes take them; stopTextInput removes the association. setTextInputArea turns the area into client pixels - logical pixels times the window's scale, dpi / 96 - and places the composition at the caret, as Dear ImGui does, and the candidate list outside the field's box, as SDL does, so the list never covers the line being typed. The committed text arrives as the window's ordinary WM_CHAR messages, the way typed text does.

In the browser nothing of this is needed: the page's own element takes the text, and the browser runs its input methods for that element and places their windows itself.

Among the other libraries, SDL has the same three requests (SDL_StartTextInput, SDL_SetTextInputArea, SDL_StopTextInput) and also reports the composition as it changes, for an application that draws it inline in its own field. sokol and raylib have no input method support - raylib through GLFW, which offers none - and Mach's core leaves it out as well. Dear ImGui positions the method's windows through its platform backend, as zimr does, and draws no composition inline either.

13.11 Files the person hands the app

An app that loads a file the person chooses, or saves one where the person chooses, works through two buttons it draws itself, Load and Save, and tells the platform each frame where they are. The reason is the browser's: a page opens its file picker, or starts a download, only from the person's own press on an element of the page, so the web platform places an invisible file input over the Load button and an invisible link over the Save button, and the press lands on them rather than on the canvas. The same areas serve every platform, so an app is written once.

   app, each frame                  platform
   draw Load at (x, y, w, h)  --->  setFilePickerArea(area, ".bvh")
   draw Save at (x, y, w, h)  --->  setSaveArea(area)      offerFile(bytes, "pose.json")

   a press inside an area:     browser  -  the invisible element takes it: picker, or download
                               Windows  -  the press is held back from the app; the Open or Save dialog opens

On Windows, the platform watches the presses it delivers: a left press inside the Load button's area opens the system's Open dialog, filtered by the names the app accepts - .bvh becomes the pattern *.bvh - and a press inside the Save button's area opens the Save dialog with the offered name. Neither the press nor its release reaches the app, as in the browser, where the page's element takes them. The dialogs are modal: the app's frames wait while one is open, and resume when it closes.

Chosen files - and files dropped on the window, or on the page - wait in a queue, oldest first. The app asks how many wait and the size of the oldest, sizes its buffer, and reads the oldest out, which removes it; a buffer too small leaves the file queued, so nothing is lost to a mis-sized buffer. A file offered for saving is copied at once, so the app may reuse its bytes as soon as it has offered them.

13.12 Networking

An app talks to a server through z.ws, the same on every platform, with the browser's model: a socket opens at once and connects in the background, its state reads connecting, open or closed, and the messages it receives wait in a queue the app drains each frame. In the browser the page's own WebSocket does the work. Natively a client in Zig speaks the protocol itself - the same protocol code the multiplayer servers use, so the client and the servers cannot disagree about a frame:

   app        z.ws.open / send / poll / close                       (every platform)
               |                              |
   browser    the page's WebSocket            native   net_core.native_websocket
                                                        a task per socket: connect, handshake, then read,
                                                        blocked until bytes arrive; a second task writes
               \                              /
   wire        net_core.websocket: frames, the opening handshake  (shared with both servers)

A native socket runs as tasks of std.Io. One connects, makes the opening handshake, then reads frames, blocked in each read until bytes arrive - no polling, so an idle socket costs nothing. A second task writes: it sleeps until there is something to write, then writes all of it - the messages the app queued (a send only queues, and returns at once, as in the browser), a pong owed, a goodbye - into one buffer sent in one write, since a frame written right behind another would wait for the other end to acknowledge the first. Closing asks the writing task to say goodbye, and gives the socket a second to end; then it cancels the socket's tasks, which std.Io does even to a read or a write blocked in the system - on Windows by cancelling the thread's synchronous I/O, elsewhere by a signal - so neither a hanging connect nor a stalled server can hold a closed socket for long.

A wss:// address is encrypted with TLS: the server's certificate is checked against the root certificates the system trusts, loaded once, the first time a secure socket connects. The TLS client brings one rule of its own: reading it can change the keys writing uses - when the server asks for a key update - so a read and a write must never overlap. The two tasks take turns with it: the writing task holds the turn for a pass, and the reading task waits for the encrypted bytes to arrive without it - so a quiet server never blocks the writing - then holds it to read one frame. Flushing is in two steps: the TLS client's flush only encrypts into the stream's buffer, and the stream's flush sends it.

A page connects to the server it came from (z.ws.originUrl). A native app came from no server, so its origin is the signaling server on the same machine, at the port the servers listen on unless told otherwise: zig build signal-server, then the app. The session layer above (z.net.Session) is the same on every platform too. Its game data travels peer to peer over WebRTC, which exists only in the browser, so a native session relays every peer through the server from the start - as a browser session does for a peer whose direct connection fails - and tells each peer so, with a relay signal: a browser in the same room then relays to it at once, instead of offering a connection the native app cannot answer.

13.13 The frame clock

A display shows frames at an even pace, once per refresh, but the moments a program measures between them jitter by a millisecond or two as the system schedules it. Animation advanced by those measured durations moves unevenly across frames shown evenly, and the eye sees it as stutter. So the duration an app advances by, f.time.delta_time, is filtered; the measured one stays available as raw_delta_time, for timing and profiling. The filter is sokol_app's, in three steps:

   measured     16.7  18.2  14.7  17.7  16.2  18.7  15.2  16.2  ...   then 8.3 8.3 ...   500
   held         (each held between 0.001 ms and 100 ms)                                  100
   filtered     16.7  16.7  16.7  16.7  16.7  16.7  16.7  16.7  ...        8.3 8.3 ...   100
                \_____ jitter: a moving average, 1/40 of the way each frame _/  \_ change of pace: adopted at once

A duration within 4 ms of the filtered one is jitter: it moves a moving average a fortieth of the way toward it. One further off is a change of pace - a display switched from 60 to 120 frames a second, a load that lasts - and is adopted at once. And each measured duration is first held between a microsecond and a tenth of a second, so that a pause in a debugger or a long hitch advances animation by one long step rather than its whole length. The average of a steady pace is that pace, so animation keeps in step with real time over any stretch; and a clock that measures the same duration every frame, as a scripted one would, comes through unchanged.

13.14 Drawing without a window

A program without a window can still draw its frames, as it would into one: the Vulkan host, started headless with a screen (startHeadlessWithScreen(width, height)), gives the engine an image to draw into in place of a window's swapchain image. The engine cannot tell the difference. The surface calls answer for the screen as they answer for a window: its size, its format (8-bit RGBA), the current texture, the present.

   with a window                              headless, with a screen

   getCurrentTextureView                      getCurrentTextureView
     acquire the next swapchain image           the frame slot's last frame done
     clear it to zeros                          clear the screen to zeros
     -> its view                                -> the screen's view
   ... the frame's passes draw ...            ... the frame's passes draw ...
   surfacePresent                             surfacePresent
     move the image to "present"                submit what is pending
     show it                                    (nothing to show it to)
     next frame slot                            next frame slot

The screen behaves as WebGPU's current texture does: transparent black when each frame first asks for it, the same texture when asked again before the present, and owned by the surface, so releasing its view does nothing. A present ends the frame: what it drew is submitted, and the frame slot moves on, so the CPU never runs more frames ahead of the GPU than it does with a window. Unlike a swapchain image, the screen is an ordinary texture that can be copied from: headlessScreenTexture() names it, and a copy into a buffer reads a frame's pixels back, exactly as it reads any texture. On a machine with no GPU, Mesa's software driver (lavapipe) runs the same Vulkan code, so frames can be drawn and read anywhere.

A whole program runs this way when it is built for the headless host: zig build hello-world-headless builds hello_world for the machine the build runs on and runs it. The program starts the host with a screen of its window's size, then calls its frames itself, as a native program does - each at a time it gives: exactly a sixtieth of a second after the one before. Every frame driver gives the engine its frame's time the same way, as update's argument - the browser its frame tick's, the Windows loop its clock's at the frame's start - and the frame clock follows it, so every run of a headless program draws the same frames; the frame clock's filter (13.13) sees the same duration every frame and passes it through unchanged. The engine's own clock, wgpu.nowMs(), stays real everywhere: it measures how long work takes, and a loop that works until a time budget is spent ends as it would anywhere.

Every flagship has such a step, zig build <name>-headless, and a run can keep frames: -Dcapture=0,30 saves frames 0 and 30 as PNG files in zig-out/headless/<name>/. A captured frame is read back from the screen just after it was drawn - the copy and read an app would make - and encoded with the engine's own PNG encoder.

SDL has a similar mode: its offscreen video driver makes windows that are never shown, for OpenGL contexts through EGL. sokol_gfx takes another approach with its dummy backend, whose functions are empty stubs: a program's calls run on the command line, but nothing is drawn. zimr's screen draws every pixel with a real driver, which is what reading frames back requires.

13.15 Input from a script

A headless run has no person at its keyboard: a script stands for one. A script is a stream of lines, each one command written in ZON - a value of the type native_input.Command, read by the standard library's own parser, so the type is the language and a new command is a new field of it. The run executes each line as it reads it. Lines that stand for a person's hands become the platform contract's own events, the ones a window delivers, so a script moves the same code a mouse and keyboard would; and one command moves time.

   .{ .move = .{ .x = 240, .y = 121 } }    the pointer moves there (logical pixels)
   .{ .press = .left }                     the left button goes down where the pointer is
   .{ .advance = 1 }                       one frame is drawn - the only command that moves time
   .{ .release = .left }                   ... the button comes up: a click, a frame long
   .{ .text = "Simon" }                    typed text - a ZON string: "\n" and "\u{e9}" are written so
   .{ .key_down = .left_control }          a key goes down, by its name in KeyboardKey
   .{ .wheel = .{ .dy = 1 } }              the wheel turns a notch away from the person
   .{ .paste = "12ab34" }                  the system's paste command, with text the app never copied
   .{ .advance = 2 }
   .capture                                the frame last drawn is saved - "captured frame_4.png"
   .{ .capture_to = "shots/name.png" }     ... at a path of its own
   .{ .sync = "7" }                        answered "synced 7" once every line before it has run
   .quit                                   the run ends, as the script's end ends it

A gamepad is read, not told of: each frame the engine asks the platform for the gamepad in every slot. So the gamepad commands make no events; they set the state that the next frame reads - a pad plugged in, a button's value, a stick's position - and keep it until changed:

   .{ .gamepad_connect = 0 }                                  a gamepad in slot 0: buttons up, sticks centred
   .{ .gamepad_button = .{ .button = .south } }               its south button down (A on an Xbox pad)
   .{ .gamepad_button = .{ .button = .right_trigger, .value = 0.7 } }   a trigger, part of the way
   .{ .gamepad_axis = .{ .axis = .left_x, .value = 0.8 } }    a stick, to the right
   .{ .gamepad_button = .{ .slot = 1, .button = .dpad_up } }  slot 1's d-pad (slot 0 when none is given)
   .{ .gamepad_disconnect = 0 }                               unplugged

Buttons and axes take the contract's names, by position. Headless, the platform's gamepads are the script's. In a window, a slot the script has played answers with the script's gamepad instead of the one plugged in - connected or empty - and a slot it has not touched still reads the real one, so a script runs alike in both. A rumble the app asks for is said in a headless run's log, which is how a script sees it.

Because only advance moves time, a run is paused whenever it is waiting for its next line, and more lines resume it - the app, its state and the GPU stay as they were. That makes a script file and a live session the same thing. A session is a script file the run follows as it grows (the ZIMR_FOLLOW setting): at the file's end the run waits for more, taking only whole lines, since a line not yet ended by a line feed may still be being written. Each step appends a few lines ending with a sync, waits for the answer, and looks at what was captured:

   session.zon  ---followed--->  the run: executes each line as it comes,   --->  replies (stdout)
   (appended to,                waits for the next one                            captured frame_3.png
    step by step)                                                                 synced 1
        ^                                                                              |
        +------------- look at frame_3.png, append the next lines --------------------+

Each frame happens at the time the run gives it - a sixtieth of a second after the one before, however long the run waited between lines - so the command file, replayed as a script, draws the same frames again: a session's transcript is a test. Replies go to the standard output, one line each; the log goes to the standard error. A line that is not a command is reported with its number and ZON's explanation, and skipped, so a session survives a typo; at the end, a run in which a line failed, the validation layer reported anything, or anything logged an error - a leak the debug allocator found among them - exits with status 1, so a script still fails as a test.

A script runs in a window too: on Windows, a program started with the ZIMR_SCRIPT setting naming a script plays it in its window - the same commands, the same frame times - and closes at its end. Its frames are shown as they are drawn, and each is kept for capture: a frame's image is copied just before it is presented, since once presented it belongs to the display. The replies go to a file beside the script, as a program with a window has no console of its own. One script, run in a window on a GPU and headless on the software driver, draws two sets of frames to compare: what differs between them is what differs between the platforms.

What a person shares with an app through the platform, the headless platform keeps as a platform does, in memory and for the run only: a clipboard the app copies to and the script pastes from, values the app saves, and the requests it grants - pointer lock and fullscreen, as a browser grants them after a click. Their outcomes reach the app as events, delivered before the next frame, as a window's loop delivers its queued messages. A key going down that is the system's paste command - Ctrl+V, or Shift+Insert - is followed by the clipboard's text, as on Windows: an app never reads the clipboard itself, it receives what is pasted.

On its way to the UI, the keyboard passes through the engine's input state, which records each key going down and coming up, and then through the UI's own snapshot, taken each frame: whether each key is held, whether it went down or came up this frame, and how many frames it has been held. Every keyboard reading in the UI - a shortcut, a held Shift, a field's editing keys - comes from that snapshot. A key meant to be held across frames goes down before one advance and comes up after it: the engine reads the keyboard frame by frame.

raylib records input in the same spirit, as automation events: a list of the events a run received, each with its frame, which can be saved as text, loaded again and replayed. A zimr script is a stream of typed commands rather than a recorded list, and runs without a window, on frames whose times the run gives.

13.16 Monitors

A platform reports the monitors a window can be on: z.monitorCount(), z.monitor(index) for each one's description, and z.currentMonitor() for the one holding the window. A description (z.Monitor) gives where the monitor's top-left corner sits on the desktop and its size, its scale, its refresh rate in hertz, the name the system gives it, and whether it is the primary one.

   the desktop, in physical pixels                    as z.monitor reports them

   +------------------------+----------------+         0: DELL U2723QE, primary
   | DISPLAY1               | DISPLAY2       |            at (0, 0), 2560 x 1440 / 1.5
   | 3840 x 2160, 144 dpi   | 1920 x 1080,   |              = 2560 x 1440, scale 1.50, 144 Hz
   | (scale 1.5)            | 96 dpi         |         1: LG 24MP400
   |                        | (scale 1)      |            at (3840, 0), 1920 x 1080,
   +------------------------+----------------+              scale 1.00, 60 Hz

Sizes are in the monitor's own logical pixels - its physical pixels divided by its scale - as everything the contract measures is in logical pixels. Each monitor has its own scale, so two monitors side by side can have different ones: a window moved from one to the other is told so by a resize event, its scale changed. Windows lists the monitors (EnumDisplayMonitors) and gives each one's rectangle and its scale, its effective DPI over 96 (GetDpiForMonitor). Its display configuration (QueryDisplayConfig) names each monitor as the monitor itself does, from its EDID, rather than by its device name (\\.\DISPLAY1), and gives the exact refresh rate as a ratio: 59.94 Hz is 60000/1001, which the display mode's whole hertz would round down to 59. Listing all this asks the display driver, which can be slow, so the monitors are listed once and kept until Windows says they changed - a monitor plugged in or out, a resolution or a scale changed (WM_DISPLAYCHANGE, WM_DPICHANGED, WM_SETTINGCHANGE). A page sees one monitor, its screen - screen.width and screen.height at the device pixel ratio - and cannot tell its refresh rate, reported as 0. A headless run's one monitor is its screen.

Because each monitor keeps its own scale, monitors side by side with different scales do not line up in one logical space: a desktop with a 1.5 monitor beside a 1.0 one has no single logical grid. A window is therefore placed on a monitor - z.setWindowPosition(monitor, x, y) puts its drawing area's top-left corner (x, y) logical pixels from that monitor's own top-left corner, in that monitor's pixels - and "centred on monitor 2" means one thing: x = (monitor.width - window width) / 2, the window's width being its drawing area's, as every size the engine gives is. The frame goes around it, its thickness taken at the scale of the monitor the window lands on. The rest of the window's requests: z.showWindow(.minimized), .maximized or .normal - a fullscreen window, covering its monitor already, does not maximize, nor move; z.focusWindow(), which Windows grants only to a program the person is using, flashing its taskbar button otherwise; and z.setWindowSizeLimits, the smallest and largest drawing area the window may have, in logical pixels. The limits apply at once - a window outside new limits is resized into them - and again each time Windows asks (WM_GETMINMAXINFO), turned into the frame's physical size at the window's own scale, so that they follow the window from monitor to monitor.

A page cannot do any of these: its browser's window is the person's to move and size. The web platform declares each request with the reason it refuses, and the refusal is a compile error - code that places a window does not build for the web, rather than silently doing nothing there. Portable code asks first: if (comptime !z.host.is_browser) z.showWindow(.maximized);.

raylib asks the same questions one at a time, from each platform's file: GetMonitorCount, GetCurrentMonitor, GetMonitorPosition, GetMonitorWidth, GetMonitorRefreshRate, GetMonitorName. zimr answers them in one description per monitor, read in a single call.

13.17 Key names

The engine names keys by their position, as the browser's KeyboardEvent.code does: .w is the key where W sits on a US keyboard, whatever the person's keyboard prints on it. Positions are what a game wants for movement - the four keys under the left hand are the same four on every layout - but not what it should show a person. z.keyName(key, buffer) gives a key's label in the person's current layout:

   position (KeyboardKey)    .w    .a    .s    .d    .q    .enter
   US layout                  W     A     S     D     Q    Enter
   French layout (AZERTY)     Z     Q     S     D     A    Entree
   German layout (QWERTZ)     W     A     S     D     Q    Eingabe

So a prompt built from the four movement keys - "move with {s} {s} {s} {s}", each filled by z.keyName - reads "W A S D" on a US keyboard and "Z Q S D" on a French one, the same four keys under the same fingers. For a letter key the label is the letter the key types in that layout; for the others it is the layout's own name for the key, in its language. On Windows, a key's position is its scancode, and GetKeyNameTextW names a scancode in the current layout. A headless run's keyboard is its script, whose keys are positions, so it reports the US labels; so does a page for now - a browser lets a page read the layout only in Chromium, through a promise (navigator.keyboard.getLayoutMap).

SDL keeps a keymap from scancodes to keys in the current layout, rebuilt when the layout changes, and names keys from it. For a layout without Latin letters - Russian, Greek - it can report the QWERTY letters instead, so that shortcuts such as Ctrl+C keep their letters. zimr keeps the two apart: shortcuts and movement are positions, which need no layout at all, and keyName is only the label shown to a person, in the letters their keyboard prints.

13.18 Gamepads

A platform answers one query per slot, gamepad(slot): the gamepad there, or null. The answer is the browser's standard gamepad - seventeen buttons by position, each from 0 to 1, and four stick axes from -1 to 1, positive to the right and down:

   contract (W3C standard order)      raylib's names, as apps ask      Xbox label
   south, east, west, north           right_face_down / right / ...    A  B  X  Y
   left_shoulder, right_shoulder      left_trigger_1, right_trigger_1  LB RB
   left_trigger, right_trigger        left_trigger_2, right_trigger_2  LT RT  (also axes 4, 5: 0 to 1)
   back, start, guide                 middle_left, middle_right, middle
   left_stick, right_stick            left_thumb, right_thumb          LS RS
   dpad_up, down, left, right         left_face_up, down, left, right
   axes: left_x, left_y, right_x, right_y                              (down positive)

Each frame, before the app's update, the engine reads every slot into its input state, so that the gamepad queries - z.isGamepadAvailable, z.isGamepadButtonDown, z.isGamepadButtonPressed, z.getGamepadAxisMovement - answer for that frame, and a press is a button down this frame and up the one before. A button counts as down from half its travel, which matters for the analog triggers; the triggers are also axes 4 and 5, for the travel itself. z.setGamepadVibration runs a pad's two motors - the heavy one on the left, the light one on the right - for a given time.

On Windows the platform reads gamepads through XInput, Xbox-style pads in four slots. Its DLL is loaded by name at first use - xinput1_4 since Windows 8, then xinput1_3, then xinput9_1_0 - as GLFW loads it, so that no version is needed for the program to start. XInput's sticks run from -32768 to 32767 with up positive, so each vertical axis is turned over into the browser's convention. Asking XInput about an empty slot is slow - it looks for a gamepad there each time - so a slot found empty is asked again only after a second: four slots read every frame would otherwise cost every frame. XInput's motors run until told to stop, so a rumble's end is kept, and the motors stopped the first time the slot is read after it. In the browser, navigator.getGamepads() already speaks the standard gamepad, and a rumble is the actuator's dual-rumble effect, which stops by itself; a page sees its gamepads only once a button is pressed while it has the focus.

A page embedded in a frame may be denied the Gamepad API: a frame's permissions policy lists the features its page may use, and an embedding viewer's sandboxed frame may leave gamepads out. navigator.getGamepads() then throws a SecurityError rather than returning a list. The bridge reads the gamepads through one function that turns that refusal into no gamepads, and says why in the console, once: such a page has no gamepads to read, so it sees none, and every other part of it runs.

13.19 Handles to JavaScript objects

The page's code is Zig translated to JavaScript, and it reaches the browser's objects - the canvas, a GPU buffer, an event, even a number passed to a browser function - through handles: indices into one array of the objects the code holds, the handle table. Every call into the browser mints handles: for its arguments, for what it returns, for each property read. A frame of a WebGPU app mints thousands. A handle must therefore be released, or the table grows with every frame until the browser refuses to make it longer.

Handles are released in bulk, by scopes. js_mark notes the table's length; js_reset releases every handle made since. Each frame runs in a scope - the whole of its tick, the request for the next frame included - and so does each event listener (scopedListener), since events arrive between frames:

   the table          boot handles | pinned | this frame's handles ......... |
                                            ^ js_mark at the frame's start      ^ the frame's end: js_reset
   after the reset    boot handles | pinned |           (the frame's slots free for the next frame)

A scope is airtight. The table also keeps a list of free slots - slots released one at a time by js_free, or around a pinned handle (below) - and a new handle takes one before growing the table. A handle a scope made in a slot below its mark would be beyond the reach of popping, so the scope notes each such slot it takes, and its reset frees those as it pops what lies above the mark.

A free slot holds a marker of its own, not undefined: a handle may hold undefined - a call that returns nothing gives one - and must still be freeable. The marker throws when it is used, so a handle used after it was freed says so at once, until its slot is given to a new handle.

A handle that must outlive its scope - an object the page keeps for its life, made the first time a frame needs it, like the canvas's context - is pinned where it is stored (Value.keep, js_pin): a reset leaves it in place, and frees the slots around it - and since a scope takes free slots first, the next frames take those slots again: a frame that pins a handle after making others lengthens the table once, not at every such pin. (Before scopes took free slots, a page whose audio a launcher restarted at each app grew by a frame's handles at every restart - some 430 a time.) js_free releases and unpins it. The two mistakes are therefore symmetric. Code that runs outside every scope keeps everything it mints: a leak. A handle stored for later without being pinned is reclaimed when its scope ends: a stale handle.

A leak shows by itself. Since every frame releases what it made, the live handles at a frame's start - the table's length less its free slots (js_live_handle_count) - are what lives outside frames: the boot handles and the pinned ones, a few hundred, and anything leaked. On a page that releases what it mints, that count stays put; on one that leaks, it rises. The length itself is not the measure: a pin made mid-frame holds it up, over free slots, with nothing kept. The bridge watches the count (watchHandleTable): a rise of 300 handles in 30 seconds - ten a second - or a count past 200 000, is written to the page's log, which the page shows on screen, naming the likely cause and the cure. The sound's diagnostics panel shows the length too (js_table_length), for the page and for the audio worklet, whose table lives in the audio thread's realm and is reset by none of the page's frames: on a healthy page both numbers hold steady from refresh to refresh.

Two faults are hard to find from a symptom alone: where a leak's handles come from, and which handle a stale use read. Handle debugging answers both (js_set_handle_debugging, switched on at run time for the page and its audio worklet - in the scale star's options, "debug handles"). While it is on, each new handle records where it was made - the transpiled functions on the stack, three deep, captured cheaply and formatted only when read - and each freed slot gets a tombstone of its own that throws naming the handle, where it was made and where it was freed:

   zimr: handle 56 used after it was freed - made at sendNotices < processBlock; freed at sendNotices < processBlock

And the live handles can be counted by where they were made (js_handle_census): the leak alarm prints the count, and the sound's panel writes it to the page's log every ten seconds - a leak shows as the code that made it, its count rising from census to census. Each record costs a stack capture, for every handle made and freed - a few microseconds, a page running somewhat slower while it is on. Off, a free slot holds the shared marker, and no stack is taken.

Promises follow the same rule. A promise's result is kept as a value until the code polling for it takes it (js_promise_take), and the handle is made then, in the taker's scope: a frame that takes a GPU readback's result has it reclaimed with the frame's other handles. A handle made when the promise settled would lie outside every scope, kept for good - one a readback, sixty a second.

One more report is always on: an exception escaping a callback - a worklet's process, a port's handler - is handed to the realm's __zimrOnCallbackError before it goes on. The browser reports an audio worklet's exception by its message alone; the worklet sends the page the exception's stack with it.

14. Sound

Sound has a tutorial of its own: Sound. It covers what this document would otherwise say about each platform's sound - WASAPI's render thread on Windows, a run with no speaker, and in the browser an audio worklet running a second instance of the app's module - together with the mixer they all play through: samples and blocks, voices and their names, the arithmetic of a block, the queues between the app's thread and the audio thread, latency from a key to the ear, and instruments.

This chapter compares the parts of it that belong to the platforms with SDL3, sokol, raylib - which plays through miniaudio - and Mach, as their sources do them.

14.1 The stream on Windows

In shared mode, WASAPI offers two kinds of stream. The standard stream (IAudioClient::Initialize) runs at the audio engine's own period, 10 ms on most devices, and Windows converts the stream's format to the device's. The low-latency stream (IAudioClient3::InitializeSharedAudioStream, Windows 10 and later) runs at a period the app chooses: a multiple of the engine's fundamental period, between the shortest and the longest the device allows (GetSharedModeEnginePeriod), in the device's own format, with no conversion. A device whose driver does not support short periods reports its default as the shortest, and the two streams then run alike.

Both streams wake their render thread once a period: the audio engine has just read a period from the buffer, and the thread has until its next read, one period later, to write the next. That period is the thread's slack against a dropout, however full the buffer is kept. How full it is kept decides only how much sound is queued ahead of a new block - the queued term of the latency readout (Sound, 8.2):

   a buffer of B frames, a period of P; at each wakeup the engine has just read P

   kept full                      the buffer's room written each time       about B - P queued ahead of a new block
   a period, when at most one     P written when no more than P is queued   at most P queued
   is left
   a period and a margin M        topped up to P + M each time              about M queued

   B 22 ms, P 10 ms, M 2 ms:      12 ms                                      at most 10 ms          about 2 ms
streamperiodwritten at each wakeup
zimrlow-latency where the device takes 48 kHz float; the standard stream otherwise the shortestthe low-latency stream topped up to a period and 2 ms; the standard stream's room
SDL3low-latency where the device has it the app's buffer size, rounded to the fundamental period, between the shortest and the longest one period, when no more than one is queued
miniaudio (raylib)low-latency where the stream needs no conversion and the period the app asks for fits; the standard stream otherwisethe app's, between the shortest and the longestone period
sokol_audiostandardthe engine'sthe buffer's room
Machlow-latency where the device has it, except under Wineleft to Windows (0) the buffer's room

miniaudio's source records the low-latency stream's limits, which the engine meets too: the stream refuses the conversion flags (AUDCLNT_STREAMFLAGS_AUTOCONVERTPCM), and asking its periods in a format other than the device's mix format fails - so a device not mixing at the engine's rate gets the standard stream, which converts.

14.2 Sound in a browser

A page has two places to compute sound. A script processor calls the page's code on the main thread, a block of 256 to 16 384 frames at a time; an audio worklet runs a small program on the browser's audio thread, 128 frames at a time. On the main thread, a long frame - a large upload, a garbage collection - delays the sound's next block as much as it delays the next picture, and a late block is a gap in the sound. On the audio thread it is not delayed.

where a page's sound is computed
zimrthe audio worklet: the renderer in a second instance of the app's own module, fed by messages (Sound, 6.4); a script processor where no worklet can load
SDL3a script processor
sokol_audioa script processor, calling the program's own callback
miniaudio (raylib)a script processor; or an audio worklet, through Emscripten's WebAssembly audio worklets, which share the module's memory with the audio thread - and so need a page served with the headers that allow shared memory

The engine's worklet shares nothing: each instance has its own memory, and what crosses between them - commands, samples, notes, a stream's frames - travels by message. A page needs no particular headers, and a page opened from a file plays the same way.

14.3 Input that starts a sound

How soon a key's sound is asked for depends on when the app hears of the key. An app that reads its input once a frame hears of a key at the next frame - up to a whole drawn frame after it went down. An app given each event as it arrives can ask for the sound at once.

how an app hears of a key
zimrits frame's input (isKeyPressed), or each press and release as it arrives (on_input, Sound, 8.1) - in a browser from the event's own handler; on Windows from the frame loop's wait, as each message arrives
sokol_appeach event as it arrives (event_cb): in a browser from Emscripten's event callbacks, on Windows from the window procedure
SDL3events polled in the app's loop (SDL_PollEvent), or one SDL_AppEvent call for each event in its callback model
raylibits frame's input (IsKeyPressed), read once a frame
